California Blvd., Pasadena, CA 91125, USA Andrew Stuart astuart@caltech.edu Affiliation: The Computing + Mathematical Sciences Department, California Institute of Technology Affiliation: 1200 E. California Blvd., Pasadena, CA 91125, USA Abstract Bayesian filtering of partially and noisily observed dynamical systems seeks to infer the evolving conditional distribution of the state of a dynamical system, given observations, in an online fashion. This Bayesian filtering distribution is the natural object for uncertainty quantification, but it is rarely available as a supervised learning target. However, one can often use the forecast model to generate synthetic system trajectories, along with corresponding synthetic observations. We introduce the proper scoring ensemble filter (PSEF), an ensemble data assimilation method based on training an analysis map to approximate the filtering distribution using only synthetic state–observation trajectories. The analysis step is represented as a permutation-invariant, transformer-based map that takes as input a forecast ensemble and observations, producing an analysis ensemble. Training is based on strictly proper scoring rules—with the energy score used in our implementation—so that probabilistic accuracy is rewarded over the whole probability distribution rather than only through the ensemble mean. We prove that, under a realizability assumption, the population objective is minimized by the true Bayesian filtering distribution. We also derive the finite-ensemble empirical objective used in training and relate its single state–observation trajectory form to the population objective, using a mean-field consistency argument. Numerical experiments show that the learned filter accurately approximates challenging filtering distributions, including nonlinear, non-Gaussian, and multi-modal posteriors, and achieves stronger performance in data assimilation tasks than classical methods or learning-based methods with mean-squared-error objectives. For close-to-Gaussian problems, learning a correction to the EnKF is the best approach, while for highly non-Gaussian problems an end-to-end approach that discards this inductive bias is superior. † † heading: 0 2026 1- –/–; Revised –/– –/– 00-0000 † † shortheadings: Learning Probabilistic Filters with Strictly Proper Scoring Rules / Bach, Baptista, Bröcker, Chen, and Stuart † † firstpage: 1 keywords data assimilation, Bayesian filtering distribution, ensemble filter, proper scoring rules, machine learning, amortized Bayesian inference 1 Introduction The Bayesian filter is defined by the sequential updating of the probability distribution of a hidden state of a dynamical system, given partial and noisy observations. Filtering algorithms, which approximate the Bayesian filter, are central to the field of data assimilation (DA). Classical sequential methods, such as particle filters (PFs) and the ensemble Kalman filter (EnKF), form the backbone of modern applications aimed at approximating the filtering problem, but face significant limitations: PFs suffer from weight degeneracy in high dimensions, requiring a number of particles that scales exponentially with the state dimension ( Snyder et al. 2008 ; Bengtsson et al. 2008 ) ; on the other hand the EnKF, which weights all particles equally and hence does not suffer from weight degeneracy, is designed to accurately approximate the true filter only when it is close to Gaussian ( Le Gland et al. 2011 ; Mandel et al. 2011 ; Carrillo et al. 2024 ; Calvello et al. 2025b ) . In recent years, data-driven approaches have emerged as a promising avenue to overcome these barriers. However, learning the true filtering distribution remains fundamentally difficult. The primary bottleneck lies in the unavailability of ground-truth filtering distributions for training. Consequently, supervised learning of the Bayesian posterior has historically been restricted to low-dimensional model problems where the ground truth is accessible. In response to these challenges, we propose the proper scoring ensemble filter (PSEF), a machine learning framework designed to approximate the true Bayesian filtering distribution. Unlike traditional approaches that rely on explicit physical assumptions or hand-crafted correction terms, the PSEF learns a parameterized analysis operator on probability measures that directly maps a forecast ensemble to an analysis ensemble. The centerpiece of our approach is a novel loss function derived from strictly proper scoring rules . This formulation enables a supervised learning paradigm that is both theoretically grounded and practically realizable: it allows the model to learn solely from ground-truth state–observation trajectories that are available through simulation, avoiding the need to access the (typically intractable) true filtering distribution during training. We provide a population-level theoretical foundation for the PSEF, based on the mean-field (infinite particle) limit of the proposed filter ( Calvello et al. 2025b ) . Under a realizability assumption, the resulting proper scoring rule objective is minimized by filters that recover the Bayesian filtering distribution. Under regularity and ergodicity assumptions, we also relate the mean-field loss arising from a single state–observation trajectory, evaluated at a realizable exact-filter parameter, to the limiting population objective. This clarifies the statistical target of the proposed objective by separating the mean-field, population limit and theory from the finite-ensemble, empirical, optimization problem deployed in practice. Furthermore, we implement a permutation-invariant architecture capable of handling varying ensemble sizes, which facilitates a computationally efficient training strategy: pretraining on small ensembles followed by lightweight fine-tuning on larger target ensembles. The practical viability of this theoretically grounded approach is demonstrated through a series of comprehensive numerical experiments on partially and noisily observed chaotic dynamical systems. In this section, we review the relevant literature in Subsection 1.1 and establish the mathematical notation used throughout the paper in Subsection 1.2 . We summarize our primary contributions in Subsection 1.3 . Finally, we provide an illustrative nonlinear filtering example in Subsection 1.4 to demonstrate that a strictly proper scoring rule objective is useful for learning non-Gaussian filtering distributions. 1.1 Literature Review This subsection reviews the literature relevant to our proposed machine-learning-based filter. Subsection 1.1.1 summarizes classical DA methods, emphasizing the Bayesian filtering viewpoint and the limitations of Gaussian and particle-based approximations. Subsection 1.1.2 reviews machine-learning approaches to DA, focusing on learned analysis operators, amortized inverse-operator viewpoints, and probabilistic training objectives. Subsection 1.1.3 briefly discusses attention-based architectures and conditioning operators defined to act on probability measures, motivating the transformer architecture used in this work. 1.1.1 Classical data assimilation Data assimilation (DA) refers to methodologies that combine dynamical models with partial and noisy observations to estimate the state of a system and often, in addition, quantify its uncertainty. In the Bayesian filtering formulation, the central object is the conditional distribution of the current state given all observations up to the current time. The filtering recursion alternates between a prediction step, which propagates the current conditional distribution through the dynamical model, and an analysis step, which incorporates the new observation through Bayes’ rule to obtain the conditional distribution at the new observation time. This perspective underlies both classical stochastic filtering and modern geophysical DA; see, for example, Jazwinski 1970 ; Doucet et al. 2001 ; Law et al. 2015 ; Reich and Cotter 2015 ; Asch et al. 2016 ; Carrassi et al. 2018 ; Evensen et al. 2022 . The Kalman filter ( Kalman 1960 ) gives the exact Bayesian filter for linear systems with Gaussian errors and Gaussian initialization ( Ho and Lee 1964 ) . For nonlinear and high-dimensional systems, ensemble-based methods such as the EnKF represent uncertainty by an ensemble of model states and approximate forecast covariances empirically ( Evensen 1994 ; Burgers et al. 1998 ; Anderson 2001 ; Houtekamer and Zhang 2016 ) . Deterministic square-root filters, including the ensemble transform Kalman filter and the ensemble adjustment Kalman filter, reduce sampling noise by avoiding perturbed observations ( Bishop et al. 2001 ; Anderson 2001 ; Tippett et al. 2003 ) . In practice, finite ensembles require regularization through inflation and localization ( Anderson and Anderson 1999 ; Anderson 2007 ; Hunt et al. 2007 ) , while nonlinear regimes have motivated iterative and optimization-based variants such as the iterative EnKF and maximum-likelihood ensemble filter ( Zupanski 2005 ; Sakov et al. 2012 ) . These methods are computationally efficient and robust, but their analysis updates remain closely tied to Gaussian or near-Gaussian approximations. Although it is provably convergent in the Gaussian and near-Gaussian setting ( Mandel et al. 2011 ; Calvello et al. 2025b ) , in general the EnKF does not asymptotically recover the true filtering distribution ( Le Gland et al. 2011 ) in the large particle limit. In numerical weather prediction, highly non-Gaussian settings have become more prominent with the move towards DA at convection-resolving scales ( Hu et al. 2023 ) , motivating research in this area. Particle filters provide a consistent Monte Carlo approximation of the nonlinear Bayesian filter ( Doucet et al. 2001 ; Gordon et al. 1993 ) , but their weights degenerate rapidly in high-dimensional systems ( Snyder et al. 2008 ; Bengtsson et al. 2008 ) . This difficulty has motivated a broad range of non-Gaussian ensemble methods, including particle flow filters ( Daum and Huang 2011 ; van Leeuwen et al. 2019 ) , transport map and normalizing flow approaches ( Spantini et al. 2022 ; Chipilski 2025 ) , and optimal transport formulations of DA ( Al-Jarrah et al. 2023 ; Bocquet et al. 2024a ) . These methods provide alternatives to Gaussian updates, but they often require solving a new transport, optimization, or sampling problem at each assimilation time. The present work follows a different route: we learn an amortized analysis operator that maps forecast ensembles and observations to analysis ensembles. 1.1.2 Machine learning for data assimilation and probabilistic training Machine learning has increasingly been used to augment classical DA methodologies ( Bach et al. 2025b ) . Examples of this intersection include the learning of model error corrections, observation operators, reduced-dimensional latent state-space representations, variational solvers, or surrogate dynamics that can be combined with classical DA pipelines ( Fablet et al. 2021 ; Frerix et al. 2021 ; Chattopadhyay et al. 2022 ; Chattopadhyay et al. 2023 ; Bach 2026 ; Arcucci et al. 2026 ) . Another line directly learns filtering or analysis procedures from data. Some previous work has considered learning filters with a specified parametric structure, such as a fixed gain ( Hoang et al. 1994 ; Bröcker et al. 2009 ; Mallia-Parfitt and Bröcker 2016 ; Levine and Stuart 2022 ; Bach et al. 2025a ) . Other work has used more flexible parameterizations based on neural networks in order to learn filtering updates in nonlinear chaotic systems ( McCabe and Brown 2021 ; Boudier et al. 2023 ; Bocquet et al. 2024b ; Hammoud et al. 2024 ; Bach et al. 2026 ) . These approaches demonstrate that learned filters can improve state estimation, especially when classical Gaussian assumptions are violated. A learned filter can be viewed as an amortized inference method: instead of solving a new Bayesian inverse problem from scratch at each assimilation time, one trains a parameterized map that can be reused across many forecast priors and observation realizations. Some methods learn both the prediction and analysis components of the filtering recursion ( Boudier et al. 2023 ; Allen et al. 2025 ) . When a reliable dynamical model is available, however, it is natural to preserve the model-based prediction step and learn only the analysis map. This analysis-only perspective is closely related to works that learn state corrections, gains, inflation, localization, or ensemble-update maps ( Bach et al. 2025a ; Bocquet et al. 2024b ; Vishny et al. 2024 ; Bach et al. 2026 ) . Most of these learned filters are trained with deterministic objectives, such as mean-square error or normalized state-estimation error. Such losses are effective for point estimation, but they generally target conditional means or task-specific summaries rather than the full filtering distribution. In this paper we target the full filtering distribution. This viewpoint is consistent with recent operator-learning perspectives on inverse problems, where end-to-end inverse maps and measure-centric probabilistic formulations provide a framework for learning observation-to-solution or observation-to-posterior maps ( Nelsen and Yang 2025 ) . Probabilistic objectives aim to learn distributional information rather than only point estimates. Variational inference provides one route to learning analysis maps from a Bayesian perspective ( Bach et al. 2025a ) ; however, the dependence on the Kullback–Leibler divergence is a limitation in high dimensions. Another route is based on proper scoring rules. A scoring rule assigns a loss to a predictive distribution after a realization is observed; it is proper if the expected score is minimized by the true data-generating distribution, and strictly proper if this minimizer is unique ( Gneiting and Raftery 2007 ) . Examples include the logarithmic score, the continuous ranked probability score (CRPS), the energy score, and variogram-based multivariate scores ( Gneiting and Raftery 2007 ; Scheuerer and Hamill 2015 ) . The fact that scoring rules assign a score to a realization, rather than a probability distribution, is especially useful in DA because simulated training data typically provide true state trajectories, but not the true filtering distributions themselves. Training with scoring rules has recently become influential in probabilistic forecasting. Generative networks for time-series forecasting have been trained by scoring rule minimization ( Pacchiardi et al. 2024 ) . In machine learning weather prediction, AIFS-CRPS trains a stochastic ensemble model using an almost-fair CRPS objective ( Lang et al. 2026 ) ; related work learns joint probabilistic forecasts from marginal distributional objectives ( Alet et al. 2025 ) ; and FourCastNet 3 develops a probabilistic global weather model with ensemble and spectral scoring components ( Bonev et al. 2025 ) . These works do not learn DA filters, since they learn forecast distributions rather than observation-conditioned analysis distributions. Nevertheless, they show that scoring rule–based objective functions can be scaled to high-dimensional ensemble-based machine learning models. The present work uses this probabilistic learning principle for the DA analysis step. Given a forecast ensemble and an observation, the learned operator outputs an analysis ensemble. Unlike deterministic loss–based approaches, the objective function is designed to recover the conditional distribution itself. Our proposed method does not require access to the true filtering distribution, which is typically not available in practice. Instead, strictly proper scoring rules allow the analysis operator to be trained against simulated truth trajectories, yielding a statistically consistent proxy for the Bayesian filtering target. 1.1.3 Attention-based operators on empirical measures An ensemble can be thought of as an empirical measure, which is naturally unordered, so a learned analysis operator should respect permutation symmetry. That is, reordering the forecast ensemble should not change the represented forecast distribution, and the output ensemble should transform equivariantly under permutations of ensemble members. Equivalently, the ensemble members should be exchangeable ( Bröcker and Kantz 2011 ) . General set architectures such as deep sets and set transformers provide standard tools for permutation-invariant and permutation-equivariant learning ( Zaheer et al. 2017 ; Lee et al. 2019 ) . In DA and related Bayesian inference problems, such symmetry-aware architectures have been used to learn ensemble update rules, neural filtering operators, and postprocessing of ensembles ( Höhlein et al. 2024 ; Zhou et al. 2024 ; Bach et al. 2026 ) . Attention mechanisms provide a natural architecture for such operators because finite attention layers compute interactions among particles or tokens through weighted aggregation. In the large-ensemble limit, this can be interpreted as an operator acting on probability measures rather than on ordered vectors. This viewpoint has been developed in recent mathematical work on transformers and attention dynamics, including the analysis of self-attention as an interacting-particle or measure-valued system ( Geshkovski et al. 2023 ; Geshkovski et al. 2025 ) , transformer-based measure-to-measure interpolation ( Geshkovski et al. 2025 ) , and continuum attention for neural operators ( Calvello et al. 2025a ) . Related notions of operator learning in the space of probability measures also appear in sampling-invariant solution-operator learning for mean-field games and in-context operator learning for optimal transport maps ( Huang and Lai 2025 ; Cole et al. 2026 ) . Recent work further formulates probabilistic conditioning as an amortized neural operator mapping joint densities to conditional densities ( Tsimpos et al. 2026 ) . These operator learning works are not DA methods per se, but they motivate the architectural viewpoint adopted here: our machine learning–based filter implements the analysis step as a permutation-invariant, attention-based operator on empirical probability measures. 1.2 Notation We write ℕ = { 1 , 2 , … } \mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\ldots\} , ℤ + = { 0 , 1 , 2 , … } \mathbb{Z}_{+}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\} , ℝ = ( − ∞ , ∞ ) \mathbb{R}=(-\infty,\infty) , and ℝ + = [ 0 , ∞ ) \mathbb{R}_{+}=[0,\infty) for the sets of positive integers, nonnegative integers, real numbers, and nonnegative reals, respectively. We use a sans-serif font (e.g., 𝖡 \mathsf{B} ) for operators acting on the space of probability measures or the space of functions; we also use this convention for operations on 𝒰 ⁡ ( [ N ] , ℝ d ) \mathcal{U}([N];\mathbb{R}^{d}) . In the context of DA, we use v v to denote the system states and y y to denote the observations. The superscript † \dagger indicates true values, and the subscript j ∈ ℤ + j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+} denotes the time step. For ensemble methods, we use the parenthesized superscript ( n ) (n) , with n ∈ ℕ , n\in\mathbb{N}, for the ensemble index. When training DA methods we use θ ∈ Θ \theta\in\Theta to denote trainable parameters, where Θ ⊆ ℝ p \Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p} is the corresponding parameter space. 1.3 Contributions and Overview The central contribution of this work is a learning framework for Bayesian filtering that targets the full filtering distribution rather than only a point estimate of the hidden state. This perspective is important because the filtering distribution is the natural object for uncertainty quantification, yet it is rarely available as a supervised learning target in realistic data assimilation problems. Our approach shows that distributional learning can nevertheless be made practical by combining ensemble filtering, strictly proper scoring rules, and permutation-invariant neural operators. Our main contributions are summarized as follows. 1. An amortized distributional learning framework for ensemble approximation of the Bayesian filter. We introduce the proper scoring ensemble filter (PSEF), a learned ensemble data assimilation method that amortizes the analysis step over forecast priors and observation realizations. 2. An objective that does not require explicit access to the Bayesian filter. We formulate the training loss using strictly proper scoring rules, with the energy score used in our implementation. This objective can be evaluated using only simulated trajectories of the state–observation process, avoiding the need to know or store the true filtering distribution during training. 3. Theoretical recovery of the Bayesian filtering distribution. We prove that, under a realizability assumption, the proper scoring rule objective is minimized by the true Bayesian filtering distribution. We further derive the finite-ensemble empirical objective used in computation and relate its single-trajectory form to the population objective through a mean-field consistency argument. 4. A permutation-invariant analysis operator on empirical measures. We implement the learned analysis step as a transformer-based map acting on empirical forecast measures and observations. This construction respects the exchangeability of ensemble members and allows the same parameterization to be evaluated across different ensemble sizes. 5. Empirical evidence across different settings. We evaluate the proposed framework on diagnostic and chaotic data assimilation problems. The results show that energy-score-trained filters approximate complex filtering distributions in settings where Kalman-type methods and mean-based learning objectives struggle, and can outperform classical methods even in settings that are not highly non-Gaussian. The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 introduces the mathematical background, including the Bayesian filtering formulation, mean-field filters, and proper scoring rules. Section 3 develops the proposed PSEF framework, establishes the population-level recovery of the true filtering distribution, derives the finite-ensemble training objective, and presents the neural analysis map and training strategy. Section 4 evaluates the method on linear–Gaussian, doubling-angle, Lorenz ’63, and Lorenz ’96 filtering problems, comparing against classical ensemble filters and learning-based baselines under both distributional and calibration metrics. Finally, Section 5 summarizes the findings, discusses limitations, and outlines future directions. 1.4 An Illustrative Doubling-Angle Example Truth ML N N ES NL2 Classical N N EnKF ESRF IEnKF N = 10 N=10 N = 300 N=300 N = 30 N=30 Training N = 1000 N=1000 N = 100 N=100 N = 3000 N=3000 — Figure 1 : Filtering distributions at assimilation step 200 for the doubling-angle model ( 1.1 ). States on the modulo-one geometry are multiplied by 2 ​ π 2\pi and projected onto the unit circle; the vertical line indicates the observed x x -coordinate. The BPF reference uses 10 6 10^{6} particles. Learning-based methods use N ∈ { 10 , 30 , 100 } N\in\{10,30,100\} , with N = 30 N=30 used for training, while EnKF, ESRF, and IEnKF use N ∈ { 300 , 1000 , 3000 } N\in\{300,1000,3000\} . The IEnKF result for N = 3000 N=3000 is omitted due to computational cost. The ES-trained model captures the bimodal filtering structure with small ensembles, whereas the NL2-trained and Kalman-type baselines miss this structure in this example. To provide concrete intuition for the proposed framework, we consider a low-dimensional doubling-angle dynamic. This is a one-dimensional model given by: v j + 1 † = 2 ​ v j † + ξ j ( mod 1 ) , \displaystyle v^{\dagger}_{j+1}=2v^{\dagger}_{j}+\xi_{j}~~(\bmod~1),\quad ξ j ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , σ v 2 ) , j ∈ ℤ + , \displaystyle\xi_{j}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\sigma_{v}^{2}),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}, (1.1a) y j + 1 † = cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ v j + 1 † ) + η j + 1 , \displaystyle y^{\dagger}_{j+1}=\cos(2\pi v^{\dagger}_{j+1})+\eta_{j+1},\quad η j + 1 ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , σ y 2 ) , j ∈ ℤ + . \displaystyle\eta_{j+1}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\sigma_{y}^{2}),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}. (1.1b) The state dynamics for { v j } j ∈ ℤ + \{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}} consists of the dyadic, or doubling, map (also known as Bernoulli map), a classic example of a chaotic and ergodic discrete-time dynamical system ( Halmos 1956 ) . The idea of this model is to double the angle on a circle at each step, with the observations { y j } j ∈ ℕ \{y_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}} being the cosine of this angle. Small deviations in the initial conditions are amplified exponentially by a factor of 2 j 2^{j} over j j steps, eventually saturating due to the fact that iterates are constrained to be in [ 0 , 1 ) [0,1) . Furthermore, the nonlinear observation model induces two modes in the resulting filtering distribution, since the states v † v^{\dagger} and 1 − v † 1-v^{\dagger} map to identical observations. Specific experimental parameters and implementation details are provided in Subsection 4.4 . Due to the low dimension, we can use a bootstrap particle filter (BPF) ( Gordon et al. 1993 ) with 10 6 10^{6} particles to provide an accurate approximation of the true filtering distribution; we use the BPF results as the ground truth to compare the performance of different methods. We aim to demonstrate that utilizing a strictly proper scoring rule allows the learned PSEF filter to accurately approximate the full filtering distribution, whereas standard losses often fail to rigorously capture statistical properties beyond the mean. We compare the classical EnKF ( Burgers et al. 1998 ; Anderson 2001 ) , ensemble square-root filter (ESRF, a deterministic square-root Kalman update) ( Tippett et al. 2003 ) , and iterative EnKF (IEnKF) ( Sakov et al. 2012 ) against two learning-based filters. For these baseline methods, we employ near-optimal inflation parameters identified via grid search, using the energy score as the optimization target. We compare these classical methods with tuned inflation against two learning-based models, which are based on the same neural network architecture but with two different loss functions: ES : Trained using the energy score loss with the power 1 1 , this underlies the PSEF methodology introduced in this paper; and NL2 : Trained using a normalized L 2 L^{2} loss, where the error is scaled by the magnitude of the true state, the state estimation loss deployed in the paper Bach et al. 2026 . As discussed in Subsection 2.3 , these loss functions differ fundamentally in their theoretical properties. Neither requires access to the Bayesian filter. However, whilst the ES loss is a strictly proper scoring rule, the NL2 loss is not proper . NL2, since it only considers the mean of the learned filter, is limited to state estimation, meaning that it cannot perform uncertainty quantification. ES, on the other hand, can achieve both. The filter trained with the strictly proper scoring loss (ES) achieves the best performance, tracking closely the theoretical optimal sampling limit for N = 10 N=10 samples. To isolate the effect of the training objective, we ensure that two learning-based methods are identical in every respect, including network architecture, optimizer, and training data, differing solely in their loss functions Figure 1 presents the filtering distributions at step 200. The true posterior exhibits a highly non-Gaussian, bimodal structure induced by the nonlinear cosine observation operator. The Kalman-type ensemble filters (EnKF, ESRF, and IEnKF) do not represent the bimodal posterior geometry in this example, even when run with substantially larger ensembles. The empirical results further expose the limitations of non–strictly proper scoring rules in learning-based filters. The model trained with the NL2 loss produces distorted posterior approximations that fail to align with the true bimodal density. In contrast, the model trained with the ES loss recovers the intricate filtering density. The ES model generalizes robustly across different ensemble sizes, capturing the precise modes and probability mass of the ground truth, whether operating with a compressed ensemble of N = 10 N=10 or scaling up to N = 100 N=100 . 2 Background In this section, we review the concepts essential for our work. We begin by formulating the filtering problem within sequential data assimilation (DA) in Subsection 2.1 . Next, we discuss mean-field filters, a relevant class of methods for this problem, in Subsection 2.2 . Finally, we introduce the theory of proper scoring rules, which underpins our proposed loss function, in Subsection 2.3 . 2.1 The Filtering Problem The DA problem setting is based on the state–observation model defined by Dynamics model: v j + 1 † = Ψ ⁡ ( v j † ) + ξ j † , ξ j † ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) ​ i.i.d. , v 0 † ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( m 0 , C 0 ) , j ∈ ℤ + , \displaystyle v_{j+1}^{\dagger}=\Psi(v_{j}^{\dagger})+\xi_{j}^{\dagger},\quad\xi_{j}^{\dagger}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma)\ \text{i.i.d.},\quad v_{0}^{\dagger}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}, (2.1a) Observation model: y j + 1 † = h ⁡ ( v j + 1 † ) + η j + 1 † , η j † ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Γ ) ​ i.i.d. , j ∈ ℤ + . \displaystyle y_{j+1}^{\dagger}=h(v_{j+1}^{\dagger})+\eta_{j+1}^{\dagger},\quad\eta_{j}^{\dagger}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Gamma)\ \text{i.i.d.},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}. (2.1b) The mapping Ψ : ℝ d v → ℝ d v \Psi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} is the deterministic forecast model, and h : ℝ d v → ℝ d y h:\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}} is the observation operator. The state variables v j † ∈ ℝ d v v_{j}^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} and the process noises ξ j † ∈ ℝ d v \xi_{j}^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} share the state dimension d v d_{v} , while the observations y j † ∈ ℝ d y y_{j}^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}} and the observation noises η j † ∈ ℝ d y \eta_{j}^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}} share the observation dimension d y d_{y} . We assume mutual independence between the initial condition and the two noise sequences, and between the noises themselves: { ξ j † } j ∈ ℤ + ⟂ v 0 † , { η j † } j ∈ ℤ + ⟂ v 0 † , { ξ j † } j ∈ ℤ + ⟂ { η j † } j ∈ ℤ + . \{\,\xi_{j}^{\dagger}\,\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\perp v_{0}^{\dagger},\qquad\{\,\eta_{j}^{\dagger}\,\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\perp v_{0}^{\dagger},\qquad\{\,\xi_{j}^{\dagger}\,\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\perp\{\,\eta_{j}^{\dagger}\,\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}. (2.2) Throughout this paper, a superscript † \dagger marks the true state or observation and their driving noises, distinguishing them from algorithmic variables introduced later, which appear without † \dagger . While we work with autonomous dynamics and additive Gaussian errors in ( 2.1 ), extensions are common in applications, such as time-varying Ψ , h , Σ , Γ \Psi,h,\Sigma,\Gamma , multiplicative or non-Gaussian noises, and infinite-dimensional state spaces for systems governed by partial differential equations. These extensions are not pursued in this paper to keep the presentation focused. Map Ψ \Psi can be interpreted as the discrete-time flow obtained by integrating an underlying continuous-time model over a fixed interval of length Δ ​ t \Delta t between observations; generalization to unequally spaced observations requires the generalization of our work to time-varying flow maps Ψ j . \Psi_{j}. Let Y j † := { y 1 † , … , y j † } Y_{j}^{\dagger}:=\{y_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,y_{j}^{\dagger}\} be the observations accumulated up to time j j . The filtering distribution at time j j is the conditional law of the state given all data up to time j j ; we denote it by π j ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) \pi_{j}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) . Definition 1 (Filtering distribution) . For each j ∈ ℤ + j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+} , the filtering distribution is π j := Law ⁡ ( v j † ∣ Y j † ) ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) , \pi_{j}:=\Law\bigl(v_{j}^{\dagger}\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}\bigr)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}), that is, the conditional probability measure of the state v j † v_{j}^{\dagger} given the observations Y j † Y_{j}^{\dagger} . For more background on filtering, see Law et al. 2015 ; Reich and Cotter 2015 ; Sanz-Alonso et al. 2023 . Definition 2 (Filtering problem) . Given the state–observation model ( 2.1 ) and the data stream { y j † } j ≥ 1 \{y_{j}^{\dagger}\}_{j\geq 1} , the filtering problem is to approximate, and update sequentially in j j , the filtering distributions { π j } j ≥ 1 \{\pi_{j}\}_{j\geq 1} . To focus our presentation we make assumptions concerning the long-term statistical behavior of the stochastic dynamics model ( 2.1a ). This behavior is determined by the interplay between the deterministic dynamics Ψ \Psi and the stochastic forcing quantified by Law ⁡ ( ξ ) \Law(\xi) . Together, the pair ( Ψ , Law ⁡ ( ξ ) ) (\Psi,\Law(\xi)) induces a discrete-time Markov chain on the state space ℝ d v \mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} , characterized by the transition kernel 𝒦 : ℝ d v × ℬ ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) → [ 0 , 1 ] \mathcal{K}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}})\to[0,1] , defined as 𝒦 ⁡ ( v , A ) := ℙ ⁡ ( Ψ ⁡ ( v ) + ξ ∈ A ) , ξ ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) . \mathcal{K}(v,A):=\mathbb{P}(\Psi(v)+\xi\in A),\quad\xi\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma). (2.3) We assume the existence of an invariant measure μ ∞ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) \mu_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) for this kernel. Definition 3 (Invariant measure) . A probability measure μ ∞ \mu_{\infty} is invariant (or stationary) with respect to the transition kernel 𝒦 \mathcal{K} (and thus the pair ( Ψ , Σ ) (\Psi,\Sigma) ) if μ ∞ ​ ( A ) = ∫ ℝ d v 𝒦 ⁡ ( v , A ) ​ μ ∞ ​ ( 𝑑 v ) for all ​ A ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) . \mu_{\infty}(A)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}}\mathcal{K}(v,A)\,\mu_{\infty}(dv)\quad\text{for all }A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}). (2.4) If v 0 † ∼ μ ∞ v_{0}^{\dagger}\sim\mu_{\infty} , the process { v j † } j ∈ ℤ + \{v_{j}^{\dagger}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} is stationary. Definition 4 (Ergodicity) . The stochastic process { v j † } j ≥ 0 \{v_{j}^{\dagger}\}_{j\geq 0} generated by the dynamics model ( 2.1a ) is ergodic with respect to the invariant measure μ ∞ \mu_{\infty} if, for any μ ∞ \mu_{\infty} -integrable test function ϕ : ℝ d v → ℝ \phi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\to\mathbb{R} , the Birkhoff average converges almost surely with respect to the law of the full stochastic trajectory: lim N → ∞ 1 N ​ ∑ j = 0 N − 1 ϕ ⁡ ( v j † ) = 𝔼 v ∼ μ ∞ ​ [ ϕ ⁡ ( v ) ] , a.s. \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\phi(v_{j}^{\dagger})=\mathbb{E}^{v\sim\mu_{\infty}}[\phi(v)],\quad\text{a.s.} (2.5) In this work, we assume the dynamics model is ergodic. Crucially for our learning-based approach, this assumption guarantees that temporal averages converge to ensemble expectations, thereby justifying the use of time-averaged losses to approximate the expected risk over the invariant distribution. 2.2 Mean-Field Filters Sequential DA proceeds by evolving the filtering distribution through a prediction–analysis cycle driven by the state–observation model ( 2.1 ). Based on the filtering distribution π j \pi_{j} , we introduce the one-step predictive law for the state and its joint extension to include the next observation: π ^ j + 1 \displaystyle\widehat{\pi}_{j+1} : = Law ⁡ ( v j + 1 † ∣ Y j † ) ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) , \displaystyle:=\Law\bigl(v_{j+1}^{\dagger}\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}\bigr)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}), (2.6a) ρ j + 1 \displaystyle\rho_{j+1} : = Law ⁡ ( ( v j + 1 † , y j + 1 † ) ∣ Y j † ) ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v + d y ) . \displaystyle:=\Law\bigl((v_{j+1}^{\dagger},y_{j+1}^{\dagger})\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}\bigr)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}+d_{y}}). (2.6b) When these laws admit densities, we use the same symbols for the corresponding density functions. The goal is to transport π j \pi_{j} to π j + 1 \pi_{j+1} by first evolving from π j \pi_{j} to the predictive distribution π ^ j + 1 \widehat{\pi}_{j+1} under the dynamics model ( 2.1a ), then extending to the joint state–observation distribution ρ j + 1 \rho_{j+1} via the observation model ( 2.1b ), and finally conditioning at the observation y j + 1 † y_{j+1}^{\dagger} to obtain π j + 1 \pi_{j+1} . Probability-map evolution The update π j → π j + 1 \pi_{j}\to\pi_{j+1} can be expressed with two linear operators, 𝖯 \mathsf{P} (forecast) and 𝖰 \mathsf{Q} (likelihood extension), followed by a nonlinear conditioning operator 𝖡 \mathsf{B} : Prediction (linear): π ^ j + 1 \displaystyle\widehat{\pi}_{j+1} = 𝖯 ​ π j , \displaystyle=\mathsf{P}\pi_{j}, (2.7a) Extend to state–observation space (linear): ρ j + 1 \displaystyle\rho_{j+1} = 𝖰 ​ π ^ j + 1 , \displaystyle=\mathsf{Q}\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}, (2.7b) Analysis (nonlinear): π j + 1 \displaystyle\pi_{j+1} = 𝖡 ⁡ ( ρ j + 1 , y j + 1 † ) . \displaystyle=\mathsf{B}(\rho_{j+1};y_{j+1}^{\dagger}). (2.7c) The initial filtering distribution is denoted by π 0 \pi_{0} ; in our experiments, unless otherwise specified, we take π 0 = 𝖭 ⁡ ( m 0 , C 0 ) \pi_{0}=\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}) . For the stochastic dynamics v j + 1 = Ψ ⁡ ( v j ) + ξ j v_{j+1}=\Psi(v_{j})+\xi_{j} with ξ j ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) \xi_{j}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma) , 𝖯 ​ π ​ ( v ) = 1 ( 2 ​ π ) d v ​ det Σ ​ ∫ exp ⁡ ( − 1 2 ​ ‖ v − Ψ ⁡ ( u ) ‖ Σ 2 ) ​ π ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . \mathsf{P}\pi(v)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d_{v}}\det\Sigma}}\int\exp\!\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\|v-\Psi(u)\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right)\pi(u)\,\mathrm{d}u. (2.8) For the observation model y = h ⁡ ( v ) + η y=h(v)+\eta with η ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Γ ) \eta\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Gamma) , 𝖰 ​ π ​ ( v , y ) = 1 ( 2 ​ π ) d y ​ det Γ ​ exp ⁡ ( − 1 2 ​ ‖ y − h ⁡ ( v ) ‖ Γ 2 ) ​ π ​ ( v ) . \mathsf{Q}\pi(v,y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d_{y}}\det\Gamma}}\exp\!\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\|y-h(v)\|_{\Gamma}^{2}\right)\pi(v). (2.9) The conditioning operator 𝖡 \mathsf{B} maps a joint state–observation law and an observed value to the conditional state law. When ρ j + 1 \rho_{j+1} admits a density, denoted by the same symbol, 𝖡 ⁡ ( ρ j + 1 , y j + 1 † ) \mathsf{B}(\rho_{j+1};y_{j+1}^{\dagger}) is the probability measure whose density is 𝖡 ⁡ ( ρ j + 1 , y j + 1 † ) ​ ( v ) = ρ j + 1 ​ ( v , y j + 1 † ) ∫ ℝ d v ρ j + 1 ​ ( u , y j + 1 † ) ​ 𝑑 u . \mathsf{B}(\rho_{j+1};y_{j+1}^{\dagger})(v)=\frac{\rho_{j+1}(v,y_{j+1}^{\dagger})}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}}\rho_{j+1}(u,y_{j+1}^{\dagger})\,\mathrm{d}u}. (2.10) We may then summarize ( 2.7 ) as π j = 𝖡 ⁡ ( 𝖰𝖯 ​ π j − 1 , y j † ) , j ∈ [ J ] . \pi_{j}=\mathsf{B}\bigl(\mathsf{Q}\mathsf{P}\pi_{j-1};y_{j}^{\dagger}\bigr),\qquad j\in[J]. (2.11) This is a nonlinear Markov process in the sense that the probability distribution π j \pi_{j} is found from a nonlinear operator applied to π j − 1 . \pi_{j-1}. Mean-field state-space evolution The nonlinear operator 𝖡 \mathsf{B} ( 2.10 ) cannot typically be computed in closed form, and numerical computations are often unfeasible for large d v d_{v} due to the high-dimensional integral involved. To implement the analysis step indirectly, a mean-field state-space evolution is introduced whose law effects the desired conditioning: Prediction: v ^ j + 1 \displaystyle\quad\widehat{v}_{j+1} = Ψ ⁡ ( v j ) + ξ j , ξ j ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) , \displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j})+\xi_{j},\quad\xi_{j}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma), (2.12a) Extend to observation: y ^ j + 1 \displaystyle\quad\widehat{y}_{j+1} = h ⁡ ( v ^ j + 1 ) + η j + 1 , η j + 1 ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Γ ) , \displaystyle=h(\,\widehat{v}_{j+1})+\eta_{j+1},\quad\eta_{j+1}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Gamma), (2.12b) Analysis: v j + 1 \displaystyle\quad v_{j+1} = 𝔅 ⁡ ( v ^ j + 1 , y ^ j + 1 , y j + 1 † , ρ j + 1 ) . \displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}(\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1};y_{j+1}^{\dagger},\rho_{j+1}). (2.12c) Here, the initial state is drawn according to v 0 ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( m 0 , C 0 ) v_{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}) . By construction of ( 2.12a )–( 2.12b ) and the definitions ( 2.8 )–( 2.9 ), we have ρ j + 1 = Law ⁡ ( ( v ^ j + 1 , y ^ j + 1 ) ) \rho_{j+1}=\Law\bigl((\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1})\bigr) . In ( 2.12c ), 𝔅 \mathfrak{B} is a vector-valued map acting on the joint state–observation space for the analysis step, parameterized by the observation y j + 1 † y^{\dagger}_{j+1} and by the joint measure ρ j + 1 \rho_{j+1} : 𝔅 ⁡ ( ∙ , ∙ , y j + 1 † , ρ j + 1 ) : ( v , y ) ↦ 𝔅 ⁡ ( v , y , y j + 1 † , ρ j + 1 ) ∈ ℝ d v . \mathfrak{B}(\,\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}\,,\,\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}\,;\,y_{j+1}^{\dagger},\rho_{j+1}):(v,y)\mapsto\mathfrak{B}(v,y;\,y_{j+1}^{\dagger},\rho_{j+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}. (2.13) The analysis operator 𝖡 \mathsf{B} acting on the probability measures is induced by 𝔅 \mathfrak{B} via pushforward: 𝖡 ⁡ ( ρ j + 1 , y j + 1 † ) = 𝔅 ​ ( ∙ , ∙ , y j + 1 † , ρ j + 1 ) ♯ ​ ρ j + 1 . \mathsf{B}(\rho_{j+1};y_{j+1}^{\dagger})=\mathfrak{B}(\,\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}\,,\,\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}\,;\,y_{j+1}^{\dagger},\rho_{j+1})_{\sharp}\,\rho_{j+1}. (2.14) With this construction, if π j = Law ⁡ ( v j ) \pi_{j}=\Law(v_{j}) , then π j + 1 = Law ⁡ ( v j + 1 ) \pi_{j+1}=\Law(v_{j+1}) . 𝔅 \mathfrak{B} is a transport map, transforming samples from the joint state–observation distribution ρ j + 1 \rho_{j+1} to ones from the filtering distribution π j + 1 \pi_{j+1} . Since it depends on ρ j + 1 \rho_{j+1} , it is a mean field map. An example: the mean-field ensemble Kalman filter A concrete instance of the measurable mapping 𝔅 \mathfrak{B} is the analysis step of the mean-field ensemble Kalman filter ( Calvello et al. 2025b ) , obtained by restricting 𝔅 \mathfrak{B} to the class of affine maps. Keeping ( 2.12a )–( 2.12b ) unchanged, define v j + 1 = 𝔅 KF ​ ( v ^ j + 1 , y ^ j + 1 , y j + 1 † , ρ j + 1 ) = v ^ j + 1 + K j + 1 ​ ( y j + 1 † − y ^ j + 1 ) , v_{j+1}\;=\;\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{KF}}(\widehat{v}_{j+1},\widehat{y}_{j+1};y_{j+1}^{\dagger},\rho_{j+1})\;=\;\widehat{v}_{j+1}+K_{j+1}\bigl(y_{j+1}^{\dagger}-\widehat{y}_{j+1}\bigr), (2.15) with gain K j + 1 = C ^ j + 1 v ​ h ​ ( C ^ j + 1 h ​ h + Γ ) − 1 , K_{j+1}\;=\;\widehat{C}^{vh}_{j+1}\bigl(\widehat{C}^{hh}_{j+1}+\Gamma\bigr)^{-1}, (2.16) where the predictive moments are taken under π ^ j + 1 \widehat{\pi}_{j+1} : v ¯ j + 1 \displaystyle\overline{v}_{j+1} = 𝔼 v ^ ∼ π ^ j + 1 ​ v ^ , h ¯ j + 1 = 𝔼 v ^ ∼ π ^ j + 1 ​ h ​ ( v ^ ) , \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\widehat{v}\sim\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}}\widehat{v},\qquad\overline{h}_{j+1}=\mathbb{E}^{\widehat{v}\sim\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}}h(\,\widehat{v}\,), (2.17a) C ^ j + 1 v ​ h \displaystyle\widehat{C}^{vh}_{j+1} = 𝔼 v ^ ∼ π ^ j + 1 ​ [ ( v ^ − v ¯ j + 1 ) ⊗ ( h ⁡ ( v ^ ) − h ¯ j + 1 ) ] , \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\widehat{v}\sim\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}}\!\left[(\widehat{v}-\overline{v}_{j+1})\otimes\bigl(h(\,\widehat{v}\,)-\overline{h}_{j+1}\bigr)\right], (2.17b) C ^ j + 1 h ​ h \displaystyle\widehat{C}^{hh}_{j+1} = 𝔼 v ^ ∼ π ^ j + 1 ​ [ ( h ⁡ ( v ^ ) − h ¯ j + 1 ) ⊗ ( h ⁡ ( v ^ ) − h ¯ j + 1 ) ] . \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\widehat{v}\sim\widehat{\pi}_{j+1}}\!\left[\bigl(h(\,\widehat{v}\,)-\overline{h}_{j+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h(\,\widehat{v}\,)-\overline{h}_{j+1}\bigr)\right]. (2.17c) Equivalently, one may write K j + 1 = C ^ j + 1 v ​ h ​ ( C ^ j + 1 y ​ y ) − 1 K_{j+1}=\widehat{C}^{vh}_{j+1}\bigl(\widehat{C}^{yy}_{j+1}\bigr)^{-1} with C ^ j + 1 y ​ y = C ^ j + 1 h ​ h + Γ \widehat{C}^{yy}_{j+1}=\widehat{C}^{hh}_{j+1}+\Gamma , making the dependence on ρ j + 1 \rho_{j+1} explicit through the synthetic observation y ^ j + 1 \widehat{y}_{j+1} . In the linear–Gaussian setting (if both Ψ \Psi and h h are linear), this affine choice yields the Bayesian posterior, and the law of v j v_{j} is governed by the classical Kalman filter ( Calvello et al. 2025b ) . 2.3 Proper Scoring Rules Probabilistic scoring rules assess how well a forecast distribution approximates a verification distribution that is only observed through a single sample. Let μ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d ) \mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) denote the forecast distribution and 𝔭 ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d ) \mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) denote the verification distribution. A scoring rule is a function 𝖲 : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d ) × ℝ d → ℝ \mathsf{S}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} , which assigns a real-valued score to a realized sample y ∼ 𝔭 y\sim\mathfrak{p} given the forecast μ \mu . We adopt the convention that scores are negatively oriented: smaller values indicate better forecasts. To compare μ \mu and 𝔭 \mathfrak{p} in expectation, we define the expected score 𝖲 ¯ ​ ( μ , 𝔭 ) := 𝔼 y ∼ 𝔭 ​ [ 𝖲 ⁡ ( μ , y ) ] = ∫ ℝ d 𝖲 ⁡ ( μ , y ) ​ 𝔭 ​ ( 𝑑 y ) . \overline{\mathsf{S}}(\mu,\mathfrak{p}):=\mathbb{E}^{y\sim\mathfrak{p}}\bigl[\mathsf{S}(\mu,y)\bigr]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{S}(\mu,y)\mathfrak{p}(\mathrm{d}y). (2.18) Definition 5 (Proper / Strictly proper scoring rule) . A scoring rule 𝖲 : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d ) × ℝ d → ℝ \mathsf{S}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} is called proper if for every verification distribution 𝔭 ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d ) \mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , 𝖲 ¯ ​ ( 𝔭 , 𝔭 ) ≤ 𝖲 ¯ ​ ( μ , 𝔭 ) for all ​ μ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d ) . \overline{\mathsf{S}}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p})\;\leq\;\overline{\mathsf{S}}(\mu,\mathfrak{p})\quad\text{for all }\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}). (2.19) Furthermore, 𝖲 \mathsf{S} is called strictly proper if it is proper and, moreover, for every 𝔭 ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d ) \mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) , 𝖲 ¯ ​ ( μ , 𝔭 ) = 𝖲 ¯ ​ ( 𝔭 , 𝔭 ) ⟹ μ = 𝔭 . \overline{\mathsf{S}}(\mu,\mathfrak{p})\;=\;\overline{\mathsf{S}}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mu=\mathfrak{p}. Equivalently, for each fixed 𝔭 \mathfrak{p} , the unique global minimizer of 𝖲 ¯ ​ ( ⋅ , 𝔭 ) \overline{\mathsf{S}}(\cdot,\mathfrak{p}) over 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d ) \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) is attained at μ = 𝔭 \mu=\mathfrak{p} . A strictly proper scoring rule makes the quantity 𝖲 ¯ ​ ( μ , 𝔭 ) − 𝖲 ¯ ​ ( 𝔭 , 𝔭 ) \overline{\mathsf{S}}(\mu,\mathfrak{p})-\overline{\mathsf{S}}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}) (2.20) a statistical divergence between μ \mu and 𝔭 \mathfrak{p} . The abstract framework above admits many concrete choices of 𝖲 \mathsf{S} . In what follows, we focus on the energy score (Definition 6 ), which is built from pairwise Euclidean distances. Definition 6 (Energy score) . Fix β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta\in(0,2) . To ensure the score is finite, the domain is restricted to 𝒫 β ​ ( ℝ d ) × ℝ d \mathcal{P}_{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d} . The energy score (ES) 𝖲 β ES : 𝒫 β ​ ( ℝ d ) × ℝ d → ℝ + \mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{\beta}:\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+} associated with a forecast μ ∈ 𝒫 β ​ ( ℝ d ) \mu\in\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) and a realization y ∈ ℝ d y\in\mathbb{R}^{d} is 𝖲 β ES ​ ( μ , y ) := 𝔼 x ∼ μ ​ [ ‖ x − y ‖ β ] − 1 2 ​ 𝔼 x , x ′ ∼ μ ​ [ ‖ x − x ′ ‖ β ] . \mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{\beta}(\mu,y):=\mathbb{E}^{x\sim\mu}\bigl[\|x-y\|^{\beta}\bigr]-\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}^{x,x^{\prime}\sim\mu}\bigl[\|x-x^{\prime}\|^{\beta}\bigr]. (2.21) For β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta\in(0,2) , 𝖲 β ES \mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{\beta} is strictly proper ( Gneiting and Raftery 2007 ) . In the one-dimensional case d = 1 d=1 with β = 1 \beta=1 , the energy score reduces to the commonly used continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) ( Bach et al. 2025b ) . Beyond the energy score and CRPS, the family of strictly proper scoring rules is extensive. We refer to Gneiting and Katzfuss 2014 ; Jordan et al. 2019 for comprehensive reviews. A classical example is the logarithmic score, defined as 𝖲 LS ​ ( μ , y ) = − log ⁡ p μ ​ ( y ) \mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{LS}}(\mu,y)=-\log p_{\mu}(y) , which is local, depending solely on the probability assigned to the realized event y y , ignoring the distance to other probable outcomes ( Du 2021 ) . In contrast, the energy score belongs to the class of non-local or distance-sensitive scoring rules ( Székely and Rizzo 2013 ) . This distance-based approach is generalized by the class of kernel scores, obtained by replacing Euclidean distances with a negative definite kernel ( Gneiting and Raftery 2007 ; Muandet et al. 2017 ) . Kernel scores quantify the discrepancy between the forecast and observation via embeddings in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space. Their expectation is mathematically equivalent to the squared maximum mean discrepancy ( Gretton et al. 2012 ) . Critically, kernel scores, which include the energy score and CRPS, since they can be written using expectations over the forecast distribution, can be evaluated directly on empirical measures ( Bach et al. 2025b ) , as we will later see in Subsection 4.1 . This makes such scores easily computable for evaluating ensemble forecasts, which motivate their use herein. Other scores, such as the logarithmic score, may require density estimation to be evaluated on empirical measures ( Bröcker and Smith 2008 ) . 3 Learning to Approximate the True Filter with Strictly Proper Scoring Rules In this section, we develop a framework for learning the data assimilation analysis operator using a probabilistic loss with synthetic true trajectories but without access to the true filtering distribution. Subsection 3.1 shows that strictly proper scoring rules allow the filtering distributions to be recovered using only the true states as training targets. We then introduce a unified family of scoring-rule objectives indexed by the time horizon, the number of independent training trajectories, and the ensemble size. When the number of training trajectories is infinite we refer to the population limit; when the number of ensemble members is infinite we refer to the mean-field limit. Subsection 3.2 connects, under regularity and ergodicity assumptions, the population and finite-trajectory mean-field objectives through a consistency result. Finally, Subsection 3.3 describes the neural particle analysis map and its practical training procedure. Auxiliary results used in the proofs are collected in B . 3.1 Filtering Distribution and Strictly Proper Scoring Rules For a strictly proper scoring rule 𝖲 \mathsf{S} , we show that the true filtering distributions are minimizers of a loss based on 𝖲 \mathsf{S} while using only the true states as the training data (Propositions 7 and 8 ). Proposition 7 . Consider a strictly proper scoring rule 𝖲 : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) × ℝ d v → ℝ \mathsf{S}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}})\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\to\mathbb{R} (Definition 5 ), and assume that all expected scores below are finite. Let π j := Law ⁡ ( v j † ∣ Y j † ) \pi_{j}:=\Law\bigl(v_{j}^{\dagger}\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}\bigr) denote the filtering distribution, viewed as a random probability measure determined by the observation history Y j † Y_{j}^{\dagger} . (3.11) ∎ In practice we use a finite-particle approximation of the mean-field limit of the algorithm, a finite-sample approximation of the population limit, and long state–observation trajectories (or a single trajectory). Nonetheless, Proposition 8 , and Theorem 12 , which follow below, provide insight into the algorithms used in practice. They shed light on the use of long state–observation trajectories (or trajectory). Indeed, Theorem 12 identifies settings in which the choice M = 1 M=1 is reasonable; we will make this choice in our numerical experiments. For each training trajectory indexed by m ∈ [ M ] m\in[M] , we use a set of particles { v j ( n ) } n = 1 N \{v^{(n)}_{j}\}_{n=1}^{N} , called the ensemble, to approximate the filtering distribution π j \pi_{j} . Here the subscript m m is omitted. To enable ensemble-based learning, we distinguish between the measure-valued analysis operator and its particle-wise implementation. We define the analysis transport map 𝔅 θ : ℝ d v × ℝ d y × ℝ d y × 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v + d y ) → ℝ d v \mathfrak{B}_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}+d_{y}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} such that 𝖡 θ : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v + d y ) × ℝ d y → 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) \mathsf{B}_{\theta}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}+d_{y}})\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) is induced by applying 𝔅 θ \mathfrak{B}_{\theta} to each particle: 𝖡 θ ​ ( ρ , y † ) = 𝔅 θ ​ ( ∙ , ∙ , y † , ρ ) ♯ ​ ρ . \mathsf{B}_{\theta}(\rho;y^{\dagger})\;=\;\mathfrak{B}_{\theta}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet};y^{\dagger},\rho)_{\sharp}\,\rho. (3.12) Keeping the prediction and extension steps the same as in ( 2.12 ), the ensemble update is explicitly defined as: Predict: v ^ j + 1 ( n ) \displaystyle\quad\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1} = Ψ ⁡ ( v j ( n ) ) + ξ j ( n ) , ξ j ( n ) ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) , \displaystyle=\Psi(v^{(n)}_{j})+\xi^{(n)}_{j},\quad\xi^{(n)}_{j}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma), (3.13a) Extend to observation: y ^ j + 1 ( n ) \displaystyle\quad\widehat{y}^{(n)}_{j+1} = h ⁡ ( v ^ j + 1 ( n ) ) + η j + 1 ( n ) , η j + 1 ( n ) ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Γ ) , \displaystyle=h(\,\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1})+\eta^{(n)}_{j+1},\quad\eta^{(n)}_{j+1}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Gamma), (3.13b) Analysis: v j + 1 ( n ) \displaystyle\quad v^{(n)}_{j+1} = 𝔅 θ ​ ( v ^ j + 1 ( n ) , y ^ j + 1 ( n ) , y j + 1 † , ρ j + 1 N ) , \displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}_{\theta}(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1},\widehat{y}^{(n)}_{j+1};y_{j+1}^{\dagger},\rho^{N}_{j+1}), (3.13c) where ρ j + 1 N \rho^{N}_{j+1} is the empirical joint measure ρ j + 1 N = 1 N ​ ∑ n = 1 N δ ( v ^ j + 1 ( n ) , y ^ j + 1 ( n ) ) . \rho^{N}_{j+1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{(\widehat{v}^{(n)}_{j+1},\widehat{y}^{(n)}_{j+1})}. This recursion is initialized using { v 0 ( n ) } n = 1 N \{v^{(n)}_{0}\}_{n=1}^{N} drawn from 𝖭 ⁡ ( v 0 † , C 0 ) \mathsf{N}(v_{0}^{\dagger},C_{0}) . In this paper, unless otherwise specified, we choose C 0 = I d v C_{0}=I_{d_{v}} . Remark 9 (Permutation invariance and varying ensemble size) . The formulation of 𝔅 θ \mathfrak{B}_{\theta} in ( 3.13c ) highlights a critical structural advantage. A naive approach treating the ensemble as a matrix in ℝ N × ( d v + d y ) \mathbb{R}^{N\times(d_{v}+d_{y})} would result in an input dimension dependent on the ensemble size N N , restricting the model to a fixed N N and requiring manual enforcement of permutation invariance. In contrast, by treating the ensemble as an empirical measure ρ N ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v + d y ) \rho^{N}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}+d_{y}}) , the domain of 𝔅 θ \mathfrak{B}_{\theta} becomes the fixed space of probability measures, independent of N N . This abstraction naturally accommodates varying ensemble sizes during training and inference. Furthermore, since the measure ρ N \rho^{N} is inherently invariant to the order of particles, the resulting map satisfies permutation invariance by design. In our implementation, we parameterize 𝔅 θ \mathfrak{B}_{\theta} using a transformer architecture ( A ), which naturally operates on set-valued inputs and respects this ensemble structure. Based on Data Assumption 1 , the practical training objective is ℒ J , M , N ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{L}_{J,M,N}(\theta) with M , N ∈ ℕ M,N\in\mathbb{N} . Compared with ℒ J , ∞ \mathcal{L}_{J,\infty} , this objective uses finitely many realized training trajectories and a finite particle ensemble. The parameter M M controls the Monte Carlo approximation of the data expectation, while N N controls the particle approximation of the mean-field filter. 3.2 Bridging the Full-Expectation and Finite-Trajectory Mean-Field Objectives In this subsection, we work in the mean-field regime N = ∞ N=\infty . We fix a realizable exact-filter parameter and show that, under ergodicity, the finite-trajectory mean-field objectives ℒ J , M \mathcal{L}_{J,M} for all M ∈ ℕ M\in\mathbb{N} and the full-expectation mean-field objective ℒ J , ∞ \mathcal{L}_{J,\infty} share the same long-time limit. The relationship between these objectives is summarized in Figure 2 . Remark 10 (Scope of finite-trajectory and finite-ensemble approximations) . For each fixed J ∈ ℕ J\in\mathbb{N} , N ∈ ℕ ∪ { ∞ } N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\} , and θ ∈ Θ \theta\in\Theta , the finite-trajectory finite-ensemble objective ℒ J , M , N ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{L}_{J,M,N}(\theta) is a Monte Carlo approximation of ℒ J , ∞ , N ​ ( θ ) \mathcal{L}_{J,\infty,N}(\theta) . Under the usual integrability assumptions, one expects lim M → ∞ ℒ J , M , N ​ ( θ ) = ℒ J , ∞ , N ​ ( θ ) \lim_{M\to\infty}\mathcal{L}_{J,M,N}(\theta)=\mathcal{L}_{J,\infty,N}(\theta) (3.14) for fixed J J , N N , and θ \theta . These fixed-horizon Monte Carlo statements are different from the long-time consistency result proved below. Theorem 12 works at the mean-field level N = ∞ N=\infty and uses temporal averaging as J → ∞ J\to\infty . It shows that, at a realizable exact-filter parameter, ℒ J , M \mathcal{L}_{J,M} converges to the same limit as ℒ J , ∞ \mathcal{L}_{J,\infty} for every M ∈ ℕ M\in\mathbb{N} on a common probability-one event. It does not assert consistency of minimizers of the finite-ensemble empirical objective ℒ J , M , N \mathcal{L}_{J,M,N} with N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N} , nor does it address convergence of neural-network optimization. Establishing such stronger statements would require additional assumptions, such as uniform-in-time propagation of chaos for the parameterized filter class and appropriate uniform laws of large numbers over Θ \Theta . To establish the convergence of the time-averaged losses, we first formulate the state space. Recall that 𝒫 1 ​ ( ℝ d v ) \mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) denotes the space of probability measures on ℝ d v \mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} with a finite first moment.
