Appendix A Attention Mechanism on Probability Measures The attention mechanism ( Vaswani et al. 2017 ) can be viewed as an operator on probability measures. Consider d u , d w , d k , d V ∈ ℕ d_{u},d_{w},d_{k},d_{V}\in\mathbb{N} and fixed matrices Q ∈ ℝ d k × d u Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{u}} , K ∈ ℝ d k × d w K\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{w}} , and V ∈ ℝ d V × d w V\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{V}\times d_{w}} . The single-head attention on probability measure is defined below: Definition 14 (Single-head attention on measures) . The attention operator is defined by 𝖠 : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) × 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) → 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d V ) , 𝖠 ⁡ ( μ , 𝔭 ) = 𝔄 ​ ( ∙ , 𝔭 ) ♯ ​ μ , \mathsf{A}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}})\times\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{V}}),\qquad\mathsf{A}(\mu,\mathfrak{p})=\mathfrak{A}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet};\mathfrak{p})_{\sharp}\mu, (A.1) where the pushforward map 𝔄 : ℝ d u × 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) → ℝ d V \mathfrak{A}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\times\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{V}} is given, for u ∈ ℝ d u u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} , by 𝔄 ⁡ ( u , 𝔭 ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{A}(u;\mathfrak{p}) = 𝔼 w ∼ p ⁡ ( ⋅ , u , 𝔭 ) ​ V ​ w , \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{w\sim p(\cdot;u,\mathfrak{p})}\,Vw, (A.2) p ⁡ ( d ​ w , u , 𝔭 ) \displaystyle p(dw;u,\mathfrak{p}) = exp ⁡ ( ⟨ Q ​ u , K ​ w ⟩ ) ​ 𝔭 ​ ( d ​ w ) ∫ ℝ d w exp ⁡ ( ⟨ Q ​ u , K ​ z ⟩ ) ​ 𝔭 ​ ( 𝑑 z ) . \displaystyle=\frac{\exp\bigl(\langle Qu,Kw\rangle\bigr)\mathfrak{p}(dw)}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}}\exp\bigl(\langle Qu,Kz\rangle\bigr)\mathfrak{p}(dz)}. (A.3) The domain requirement 𝔭 ∈ 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) \mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}) (Definition 15 ) guarantees the normalizing denominator in ( A.3 ) is finite for every u ∈ ℝ d u u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} ( Bach et al. 2026 ) , hence p ⁡ ( ⋅ , u , 𝔭 ) p(\cdot;u,\mathfrak{p}) is well-defined and 𝖠 ⁡ ( μ , 𝔭 ) ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d V ) \mathsf{A}(\mu,\mathfrak{p})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{V}}) for all μ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) \mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}) . Definition 15 (Set of Measures with Fast Tail Decay) . Define 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d ) \mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) as the set of probability measures on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} whose tails decay faster than exponentially. More precisely, 𝒫 + ( ℝ d ) = { 𝔭 ∈ 𝒫 ( ℝ d ) : \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\Bigl\{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}): ∃ c 𝔭 &gt; 0 , R 𝔭 ≥ 0 , δ 𝔭 &gt; 0 ​ such that \displaystyle\exists c_{\mathfrak{p}}&gt;0,\ R_{\mathfrak{p}}\geq 0,\ \delta_{\mathfrak{p}}&gt;0\text{ such that} (A.4) 𝔭 ( { x ∈ ℝ d : ∥ x ∥ ≥ t } ) ≤ exp ( − c 𝔭 t 1 + δ 𝔭 ) ∀ t ≥ R 𝔭 } . \displaystyle\mathfrak{p}\bigl(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x\|\geq t\}\bigr)\leq\exp\bigl(-c_{\mathfrak{p}}t^{1+\delta_{\mathfrak{p}}}\bigr)\quad\forall t\geq R_{\mathfrak{p}}\Bigr\}. The following Proposition 16 shows that with empirical-measure inputs, the attention operator 𝖠 \mathsf{A} coincides with standard sequence-based attention ( Vaswani et al. 2017 ) . Proposition 16 (Attention on Empirical Measures) . Let μ N = 1 N ​ ∑ j = 1 N δ u ⁡ ( j ) \mu^{N}=\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\delta_{u(j)} , 𝔭 M = 1 M ​ ∑ k = 1 M δ w ⁡ ( k ) \mathfrak{p}^{M}=\tfrac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\delta_{w(k)} , Q ∈ ℝ d k × d u Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{u}} , K ∈ ℝ d k × d w K\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{w}} , and V ∈ ℝ d V × d w V\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{V}\times d_{w}} . We have 𝖠 ⁡ ( μ N , 𝔭 M ) = 1 N ​ ∑ j = 1 N δ 𝔄 ⁡ ( u ⁡ ( j ) , 𝔭 M ) \mathsf{A}(\mu^{N},\mathfrak{p}^{M})=\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\delta_{\mathfrak{A}(u(j);\mathfrak{p}^{M})} , where 𝔄 ⁡ ( u ⁡ ( j ) , 𝔭 M ) = ∑ k = 1 M exp ⁡ ⟨ Q ​ u ​ ( j ) , K ​ w ​ ( k ) ⟩ ​ V ​ w ​ ( k ) ∑ ℓ = 1 M exp ⁡ ⟨ Q ​ u ​ ( j ) , K ​ w ​ ( ℓ ) ⟩ . \mathfrak{A}\bigl(u(j);\mathfrak{p}^{M}\bigr)=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{M}\exp\langle Qu(j),Kw(k)\rangle\,Vw(k)}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{M}\exp\langle Qu(j),Kw(\ell)\rangle}. On the other hand, stacking { u ⁡ ( j ) } j = 1 N \{u(j)\}_{j=1}^{N} into U ∈ ℝ N × d u U\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d_{u}} and { w ⁡ ( k ) } k = 1 M \{w(k)\}_{k=1}^{M} into W ∈ ℝ M × d w W\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times d_{w}} , the classic attention is 𝖠𝗍𝗍𝖾𝗇𝗍𝗂𝗈𝗇 ⁡ ( U , W ) = Softmax ⁡ ( ( U ​ Q ⊤ ) ​ ( W ​ K ⊤ ) ⊤ ) ​ ( W ​ V ⊤ ) ∈ ℝ N × d V \mathsf{Attention}(U,W)=\mathrm{Softmax}\bigl((UQ^{\top})(WK^{\top})^{\top}\bigr)\,(WV^{\top})\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d_{V}} For each j ∈ { 1 , … , N } j\in\{1,\dots,N\} , 𝔄 ⁡ ( u ⁡ ( j ) , 𝔭 M ) \mathfrak{A}\bigl(u(j);\mathfrak{p}^{M}\bigr) is the row j j of 𝖠𝗍𝗍𝖾𝗇𝗍𝗂𝗈𝗇 ⁡ ( U , W ) \mathsf{Attention}(U,W) . Proof. Directly substitute the empirical measures μ N = 1 N ​ ∑ j = 1 N δ u ⁡ ( j ) , n ​ u M = 1 M ​ ∑ k = 1 M δ w ⁡ ( k ) \mu^{N}=\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\delta_{u(j)},nu^{M}=\tfrac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\delta_{w(k)} into ( A.1 )–( A.3 ). ∎ While Definition 14 introduces the single-head attention operator, modern transformer architectures typically employ Multi-Head Attention (MHA) ( Vaswani et al. 2017 ) to jointly attend to information from different representation subspaces. We can naturally extend the attention on measures to the multi-head setting. Definition 17 (Multi-Head Attention on Measures) . Let H ∈ ℕ H\in\mathbb{N} be the number of attention heads. For each head h ∈ { 1 , … , H } h\in\{1,\dots,H\} , let Q h ∈ ℝ d k × d u Q_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{u}} , K h ∈ ℝ d k × d w K_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{w}} , and V h ∈ ℝ d V × d w V_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{V}\times d_{w}} be the specific weight matrices. Each head induces a single-head pushforward map 𝔄 h : ℝ d u × 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) → ℝ d V \mathfrak{A}_{h}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\times\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{V}} given strictly by ( A.2 )–( A.3 ). Given an output projection matrix W O ∈ ℝ d u × ( H ​ d V ) W_{O}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}\times(Hd_{V})} , the multi-head attention map 𝔄 MHA : ℝ d u × 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) → ℝ d u \mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{MHA}}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\times\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} is defined by concatenating the head outputs and applying the linear projection: 𝔄 MHA ​ ( u , 𝔭 ) = W O ​ [ 𝔄 1 ​ ( u , 𝔭 ) 𝔄 H ​ ( u , 𝔭 ) ] . \mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{MHA}}(u;\mathfrak{p})=W_{O}\begin{bmatrix}\mathfrak{A}_{1}(u;\mathfrak{p})\\ \vdots\\ \mathfrak{A}_{H}(u;\mathfrak{p})\end{bmatrix}. (A.5) The multi-head attention operator 𝖠 MHA : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) × 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) → 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) \mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{MHA}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}})\times\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}) is then defined via the pushforward operation: 𝖠 MHA ​ ( μ , 𝔭 ) = 𝔄 MHA ​ ( ∙ , 𝔭 ) ♯ ​ μ . \mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{MHA}}(\mu,\mathfrak{p})=\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{MHA}}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet};\mathfrak{p})_{\sharp}\mu. (A.6) In practice, the sequence-based multi-head attention is combined with other layers to form a transformer block. We define the transformer block 𝖳 \mathsf{T} on measures as Definition 18 . Note that the output projection W O W_{O} in MHA perfectly maps the attention output back to ℝ d u \mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} , which dimensionally aligns the subsequent residual connection. Definition 18 (Transformer Block on Measures) . Based on the multi-head attention on measures 𝖠 MHA \mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{MHA}} (Definition 17 ), the transformer block 𝖳 : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) × 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) → 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) \mathsf{T}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}})\times\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}) is defined via a pushforward operation under a transport map 𝔗 \mathfrak{T} : we define 𝖳 ⁡ ( μ , 𝔭 ) = 𝔗 ​ ( ∙ , 𝔭 ) ♯ ​ μ . \mathsf{T}(\mu,\mathfrak{p})=\mathfrak{T}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet};\mathfrak{p})_{\sharp}\mu. (A.7) The map 𝔗 : ℝ d u × 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) → ℝ d u \mathfrak{T}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\times\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} acting on the inputs u ∈ ℝ d u u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} and 𝔭 ∈ 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) \mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}) is defined as the composition of the maps: u 1 \displaystyle u_{1} = 𝔉 LN ​ ( u + 𝔄 MHA ​ ( u , 𝔭 ) ) , \displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}^{\textrm{LN}}\bigl(u+\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{MHA}}(u;\mathfrak{p})\bigr), (A.8a) u 2 \displaystyle u_{2} = 𝔉 LN ​ ( u 1 + 𝔉 NN ​ ( u 1 ) ) , \displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}^{\textrm{LN}}\bigl(u_{1}+\mathfrak{F}^{\textrm{NN}}(u_{1})\bigr), (A.8b) 𝔗 ⁡ ( u , 𝔭 ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{T}(u,\mathfrak{p}) = u 2 . \displaystyle=u_{2}. (A.8c) The map 𝔉 LN : ℝ d u → ℝ d u \mathfrak{F}^{\textrm{LN}}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} defines layer normalization and is such that ( 𝔉 LN ​ ( u , γ , β ) ) k = γ k ⋅ u k − m ⁡ ( u ) σ 2 ​ ( u ) + ε + β k , \Bigl(\mathfrak{F}^{\textrm{LN}}({u};\gamma,\beta)\Bigr)_{k}=\gamma_{k}\cdot\frac{u_{k}-m(u)}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}(u)+\varepsilon}}+\beta_{k}, (A.9) for k = 1 , … , d u k=1,\dots,d_{u} , any u ∈ ℝ d u u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} , where the subscript notation ( ∙ ) k (\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})_{k} is used to denote the k k ’th entry of the vector. In equation ( A.9 ), ε ∈ ℝ + \varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+} is a fixed parameter, γ k , β k ∈ ℝ \gamma_{k},\beta_{k}\in\mathbb{R} are learnable parameters and m , σ m,\sigma are defined as m ( u ) = 1 d u ∑ k = 1 d u u k , σ 2 ( u ) = 1 d u ∑ k = 1 d u ( u k − m ( u ) ) 2 , \displaystyle m(u)=\frac{1}{d_{u}}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{u}}u_{k},\qquad\sigma^{2}(u)=\frac{1}{d_{u}}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{u}}\bigl(u_{k}-m(u)\bigr)^{2}, (A.10) for any u ∈ ℝ d u . u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}. The operator 𝔉 NN : ℝ d u → ℝ d u \mathfrak{F}^{\textrm{NN}}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} is defined such that 𝔉 NN ​ ( u , W 1 , W 2 , b 1 , b 2 ) = W 2 ​ f ​ ( W 1 ​ u + b 1 ) + b 2 , \mathfrak{F}^{\textrm{NN}}(u;W_{1},W_{2},b_{1},b_{2})=W_{2}f\bigl(W_{1}u+b_{1}\bigr)+b_{2}, (A.11) for any u ∈ ℝ d u u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} , where W 1 , W 2 ∈ ℝ d u × d u W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}\times d_{u}} and b 1 , b 2 ∈ ℝ d u b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} are learnable parameters and where f f is a nonlinear activation function. In our notation we distinguish a self-attention block 𝖳 s : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) → 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) \mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{s}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}) , 𝖳 s ​ ( μ ) = 𝖳 ⁡ ( μ , μ ) \mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mu)=\mathsf{T}(\mu,\mu) , and a cross-attention block 𝖳 c : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) × 𝒫 + ​ ( ℝ d w ) → 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) \mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{c}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}})\times\mathcal{P}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}) , 𝖳 c ​ ( μ , 𝔭 ) = 𝖳 ⁡ ( μ , 𝔭 ) \mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mu,\mathfrak{p})=\mathsf{T}(\mu,\mathfrak{p}) , built from the same transformer map 𝔗 \mathfrak{T} in Definition 18 . The transformer measure neural mapping (TMNM) ( Bach et al. 2026 ) , which is an extension of the set transformer ( Lee et al. 2019 ) , maps a probability measure into a fixed-dimension feature vector (or a matrix). The TMNM 𝖥 TMNM : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d w ) → ℝ N s × d u \mathsf{F}^{\mathrm{TMNM}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}})\to\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}\times d_{u}} is given by: 𝖥 TMNM ( μ ) = 𝖥 Cat ( ∙ ) ∘ 𝖳 d , N d + N e s ( ∙ ) ∘ ⋯ ∘ 𝖳 d , 1 + N e s ( ∙ ) ∘ 𝖳 c ( 𝔭 N s , ∙ ) ∘ 𝖳 e , N e s ( ∙ ) ∘ ⋯ ∘ 𝖳 e , 2 s ( ∙ ) ∘ 𝖳 e , 1 s ( μ ) , \mathsf{F}^{\mathrm{TMNM}}(\mu)=\mathsf{F}^{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\circ\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{s}}_{d,N_{d}+N_{e}}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\circ\cdots\circ\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{s}}_{d,1+N_{e}}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\circ\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{c}}(\mathfrak{p}^{N_{s}},\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\circ\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{s}}_{e,N_{e}}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\circ\cdots\circ\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{s}}_{e,2}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\circ\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{s}}_{e,1}(\mu), (A.12) where 1. 𝖥 Cat \mathsf{F}^{\mathrm{Cat}} maps an empirical measure supported on N N points in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} to the matrix in ℝ N × d \mathbb{R}^{N\times d} obtained by stacking the support points as rows. 2. In the subscript ( k , ℓ ) (k,\ell) of the self-attention blocks 𝖳 k , ℓ s \mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{s}}_{k,\ell} in ( A.12 ), the index k ∈ { e , d } k\in\{e,d\} indicates whether the block belongs to the encoder ( k = e ) (k=e) or the decoder ( k = d ) (k=d) . For encoder blocks we use ℓ = 1 , … , N e \ell=1,\ldots,N_{e} , and for decoder blocks we use ℓ = 1 + N e , … , N e + N d \ell=1+N_{e},\ldots,N_{e}+N_{d} . 3. The cross-attention block 𝖳 c \mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{c}} links encoder and decoder. Its first argument 𝔭 N s ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d u ) \mathfrak{p}^{N_{s}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}) is an empirical measure supported on N s N_{s} learnable points in ℝ d u \mathbb{R}^{d_{u}} , where N s N_{s} is a fixed hyperparameter that determines the size of the output feature matrix. 4. The trainable parameters consist of all parameters in the transformer blocks together with the N s N_{s} learnable support points defining 𝔭 N s \mathfrak{p}^{N_{s}} . Appendix B Auxiliary Results To contextualize the lemmas presented in this section, we briefly recall the augmented state space formulation introduced in the main text. Note that 𝒫 k ​ ( ℝ d ) \mathcal{P}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) denotes the space of probability measures on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} with finite k k -th moments Consider Ω := 𝒫 1 ​ ( ℝ d v ) × ℝ d v \Omega:=\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}})\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} , equipped with the metric D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) = W 1 ​ ( q , q ′ ) + ‖ v − v ′ ‖ , \mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})=W_{1}(q,q^{\prime})+\|v-v^{\prime}\|, (B.1) for any Z = ( q , v ) Z=(q,v) and Z ′ = ( q ′ , v ′ ) Z^{\prime}=(q^{\prime},v^{\prime}) in Ω \Omega . Within this metric space, we investigate the augmented process, defined as Z j := ( π j , v j † ) , Z_{j}:=\bigl(\pi_{j},\,v_{j}^{\dagger}\bigr), (B.2) where π j = Law ⁡ ( v j † ∣ Y j † ) \pi_{j}=\Law\bigl(v_{j}^{\dagger}\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}\bigr) is the true filtering distribution. We denote its law as μ j := Law ⁡ ( Z j ) ∈ 𝒫 1 ​ ( Ω ) \mu_{j}:=\Law(Z_{j})\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\Omega) . Based on this formulation, we introduce auxiliary lemmas regarding the properties of the energy score and the convergence of expected sequence scores. B.1 Lipschitz Continuity of the Energy Score We first provide a result on the Lipschitz continuity of the energy score. We recall the definition of the energy score (Definition 6 ). Lemma 19 (Lipschitz Continuity of the Energy Score with β = 1 \beta=1 ) . The energy score (Definition 6 ) with β = 1 \beta=1 is Lipschitz continuous on Ω \Omega with a constant C 𝖲 = 2 C_{\mathsf{S}}=2 . That is, for 𝖲 = 𝖲 1 ES \mathsf{S}=\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1} , | 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z ) − 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z ′ ) | ≤ 2 ​ D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) . |\mathsf{S}(Z)-\mathsf{S}(Z^{\prime})|\leq 2\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime}). (B.3) Proof. Let Z = ( q , v ) Z=(q,v) and Z ′ = ( q ′ , v ′ ) Z^{\prime}=(q^{\prime},v^{\prime}) be elements in Ω \Omega . By the triangle inequality, the difference in the scoring rule evaluates to | 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( Z ) − 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( Z ′ ) | ≤ | 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q , v ) − 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q ′ , v ) | + | 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q ′ , v ) − 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q ′ , v ′ ) | . |\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(Z)-\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(Z^{\prime})|\leq|\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q,v)-\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q^{\prime},v)|+|\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q^{\prime},v)-\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q^{\prime},v^{\prime})|. (B.4) For the first term, expanding the definition of the energy score yields | 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q , v ) − 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q ′ , v ) | ≤ | 𝔼 x ∼ q ​ [ ‖ x − v ‖ ] − 𝔼 y ∼ q ′ ​ [ ‖ y − v ‖ ] | + 1 2 ​ | 𝔼 x , x ′ ∼ q ​ [ ‖ x − x ′ ‖ ] − 𝔼 y , y ′ ∼ q ′ ​ [ ‖ y − y ′ ‖ ] | . |\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q,v)-\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q^{\prime},v)|\leq\left|\mathbb{E}^{x\sim q}[\|x-v\|]-\mathbb{E}^{y\sim q^{\prime}}[\|y-v\|]\right|\\ +\frac{1}{2}\left|\mathbb{E}^{x,x^{\prime}\sim q}[\|x-x^{\prime}\|]-\mathbb{E}^{y,y^{\prime}\sim q^{\prime}}[\|y-y^{\prime}\|]\right|. (B.5) The function f ⁡ ( x ) = ‖ x − v ‖ f(x)=\|x-v\| is 1 1 -Lipschitz with respect to the Euclidean norm. By the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality theorem, it follows that | 𝔼 x ∼ q ​ [ ‖ x − v ‖ ] − 𝔼 y ∼ q ′ ​ [ ‖ y − v ‖ ] | ≤ W 1 ​ ( q , q ′ ) . \left|\mathbb{E}^{x\sim q}[\|x-v\|]-\mathbb{E}^{y\sim q^{\prime}}[\|y-v\|]\right|\leq W_{1}(q,q^{\prime}). (B.6) To bound the interaction term, let 𝔭 ∈ Π ⁡ ( q , q ′ ) \mathfrak{p}\in\Pi(q,q^{\prime}) be an optimal coupling such that 𝔼 ( x , y ) ∼ 𝔭 ​ [ ‖ x − y ‖ ] = W 1 ​ ( q , q ′ ) \mathbb{E}^{(x,y)\sim\mathfrak{p}}[\|x-y\|]=W_{1}(q,q^{\prime}) . Let 𝔭 ⊗ 𝔭 \mathfrak{p}\otimes\mathfrak{p} denote the joint distribution of two independent draws from 𝔭 \mathfrak{p} , where ⊗ \otimes denotes the product of measures. Let ( X , Y ) (X,Y) and ( X ′ , Y ′ ) (X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}) be two such independent pairs of random variables distributed according to 𝔭 \mathfrak{p} . Under this construction, the marginal distributions satisfy ( X , X ′ ) ∼ q ⊗ q (X,X^{\prime})\sim q\otimes q and ( Y , Y ′ ) ∼ q ′ ⊗ q ′ (Y,Y^{\prime})\sim q^{\prime}\otimes q^{\prime} . The triangle inequality guarantees | ‖ X − X ′ ‖ − ‖ Y − Y ′ ‖ | ≤ ‖ X − Y ‖ + ‖ X ′ − Y ′ ‖ \bigl|\|X-X^{\prime}\|-\|Y-Y^{\prime}\|\bigr|\leq\|X-Y\|+\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\| . It follows that the difference of the interaction expectations is bounded by | 𝔼 x , x ′ ∼ q ​ [ ‖ x − x ′ ‖ ] − 𝔼 y , y ′ ∼ q ′ ​ [ ‖ y − y ′ ‖ ] | \displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}^{x,x^{\prime}\sim q}[\|x-x^{\prime}\|]-\mathbb{E}^{y,y^{\prime}\sim q^{\prime}}[\|y-y^{\prime}\|]\right| = | 𝔼 ( X , Y ) , ( X ′ , Y ′ ) ∼ 𝔭 ⊗ 𝔭 ​ [ ‖ X − X ′ ‖ − ‖ Y − Y ′ ‖ ] | \displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}^{(X,Y),(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\sim\mathfrak{p}\otimes\mathfrak{p}}\bigl[\|X-X^{\prime}\|-\|Y-Y^{\prime}\|\bigr]\right| (B.7) ≤ 𝔼 ( X , Y ) , ( X ′ , Y ′ ) ∼ 𝔭 ⊗ 𝔭 ​ [ ‖ X − Y ‖ + ‖ X ′ − Y ′ ‖ ] \displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{(X,Y),(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\sim\mathfrak{p}\otimes\mathfrak{p}}\bigl[\|X-Y\|+\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|\bigr] = 2 ​ 𝔼 ( X , Y ) ∼ 𝔭 ​ [ ‖ X − Y ‖ ] = 2 ​ W 1 ​ ( q , q ′ ) . \displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}^{(X,Y)\sim\mathfrak{p}}\bigl[\|X-Y\|\bigr]=2W_{1}(q,q^{\prime}). Combining these two bounds, we obtain | 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q , v ) − 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q ′ , v ) | ≤ W 1 ​ ( q , q ′ ) + 1 2 ​ ( 2 ​ W 1 ​ ( q , q ′ ) ) = 2 ​ W 1 ​ ( q , q ′ ) . |\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q,v)-\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q^{\prime},v)|\leq W_{1}(q,q^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\bigl(2W_{1}(q,q^{\prime})\bigr)=2W_{1}(q,q^{\prime}). (B.8) For the second term, the pairwise distance expectations under q ′ q^{\prime} cancel out, leaving | 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q ′ , v ) − 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( q ′ , v ′ ) | = | 𝔼 x ∼ q ′ ​ [ ‖ x − v ‖ ] − 𝔼 x ∼ q ′ ​ [ ‖ x − v ′ ‖ ] | ≤ 𝔼 x ∼ q ′ ​ [ ‖ v − v ′ ‖ ] = ‖ v − v ′ ‖ . |\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q^{\prime},v)-\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(q^{\prime},v^{\prime})|=\left|\mathbb{E}^{x\sim q^{\prime}}[\|x-v\|]-\mathbb{E}^{x\sim q^{\prime}}[\|x-v^{\prime}\|]\right|\leq\mathbb{E}^{x\sim q^{\prime}}\bigl[\|v-v^{\prime}\|\bigr]=\|v-v^{\prime}\|. (B.9) Therefore, | 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( Z ) − 𝖲 1 ES ​ ( Z ′ ) | ≤ 2 ​ W 1 ​ ( q , q ′ ) + ‖ v − v ′ ‖ ≤ 2 ​ D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) . |\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(Z)-\mathsf{S}^{\mathrm{ES}}_{1}(Z^{\prime})|\leq 2W_{1}(q,q^{\prime})+\|v-v^{\prime}\|\leq 2\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime}). (B.10) ∎ B.2 Convergence Properties of Expected Scores Based on the augmented space formulation, we introduce two lemmas below to analytically support the convergence propositions established in the main text. Lemma 20 (Change of Variables for Expected Score) . Let μ j = Law ⁡ ( Z j ) ∈ 𝒫 1 ​ ( Ω ) \mu_{j}=\Law(Z_{j})\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\Omega) for j ∈ ℤ + j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+} . Then for any measurable scoring rule 𝖲 : Ω → ℝ + \mathsf{S}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+} , we have 𝔼 ( v j † , Y j † ) ​ [ 𝖲 ⁡ ( π j , v j † ) ] = 𝔼 ( q , v ) ∼ μ j ​ [ 𝖲 ⁡ ( q , v ) ] . \mathbb{E}^{(v_{j}^{\dagger},Y_{j}^{\dagger})}\left[\mathsf{S}(\pi_{j},v_{j}^{\dagger})\right]=\mathbb{E}^{(q,v)\sim\mu_{j}}\left[\mathsf{S}(q,v)\right]. (B.11) Proof. It suffices to note that there exists a measurable map 𝖥 j : ∏ k = 1 j ℝ d y → 𝒫 1 ​ ( ℝ d v ) , s.t. ​ π j = 𝖥 j ​ ( Y j † ) . \mathsf{F}_{j}:\prod_{k=1}^{j}\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}\to\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}),\qquad\text{s.t. }\pi_{j}=\mathsf{F}_{j}(Y_{j}^{\dagger}). (B.12) Indeed, since the exact filter is initialized at π 0 \pi_{0} and updated recursively by π ℓ + 1 = 𝖡 ⁡ ( 𝖰𝖯 ​ π ℓ , y ℓ + 1 † ) , ℓ ∈ ℤ + , \pi_{\ell+1}=\mathsf{B}(\mathsf{Q}\mathsf{P}\pi_{\ell};y_{\ell+1}^{\dagger}),\qquad\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}, (B.13) the filtering distribution π j \pi_{j} is deterministically given by the observation history sequence Y j † = ( y 1 † , … , y j † ) Y_{j}^{\dagger}=(y_{1}^{\dagger},\ldots,y_{j}^{\dagger}) . Therefore, we can write 𝖥 j \mathsf{F}_{j} explicitly as the composition 𝖥 j ( y 1 † , … , y j † ) = 𝖡 ( 𝖰𝖯 𝖡 ( ⋯ 𝖡 ( 𝖰𝖯 π 0 ; y 1 † ) ⋯ ; y j − 1 † ) ; y j † ) . \mathsf{F}_{j}(y_{1}^{\dagger},\ldots,y_{j}^{\dagger})=\mathsf{B}\Bigl(\mathsf{Q}\mathsf{P}\,\mathsf{B}\bigl(\cdots\mathsf{B}(\mathsf{Q}\mathsf{P}\pi_{0};y_{1}^{\dagger})\cdots;y_{j-1}^{\dagger}\bigr);y_{j}^{\dagger}\Bigr). (B.14) Because the augmented state Z j = ( π j , v j † ) = ( 𝖥 j ​ ( Y j † ) , v j † ) Z_{j}=(\pi_{j},v_{j}^{\dagger})=\bigl(\mathsf{F}_{j}(Y_{j}^{\dagger}),v_{j}^{\dagger}\bigr) is completely determined by the random variables ( v j † , Y j † ) (v_{j}^{\dagger},Y_{j}^{\dagger}) , taking the expectation of 𝖲 ⁡ ( π j , v j † ) \mathsf{S}(\pi_{j},v_{j}^{\dagger}) with respect to the joint law of the physical state and observations is strictly equivalent to taking the expectation of 𝖲 ⁡ ( q , v ) \mathsf{S}(q,v) with respect to the induced push-forward measure μ j = Law ⁡ ( Z j ) \mu_{j}=\Law(Z_{j}) by the law of the unconscious statistician. ∎ Lemma 21 (Invariant Integrability and Integral Convergence) . Let { 𝔭 j } j ∈ ℤ + \{\mathfrak{p}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} be a sequence of probability measures in 𝒫 1 ​ ( Ω ) \mathcal{P}_{1}(\Omega) that converges to a limiting measure 𝔭 ∞ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mathfrak{p}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega) in the Wasserstein-1 distance, i.e., lim j → ∞ W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 j , 𝔭 ∞ ) = 0 \lim_{j\to\infty}W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{j},\mathfrak{p}_{\infty})=0 . If a scoring rule 𝖲 : Ω → ℝ + \mathsf{S}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+} is non-negative and Lipschitz continuous with constant C 𝖲 C_{\mathsf{S}} with respect to D Ω \mathrm{D}_{\Omega} , then 𝔭 ∞ ∈ 𝒫 1 ​ ( Ω ) \mathfrak{p}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\Omega) , the scoring rule 𝖲 \mathsf{S} is integrable with respect to both 𝔭 j \mathfrak{p}_{j} for j ∈ ℤ + j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+} and 𝔭 ∞ \mathfrak{p}_{\infty} , and we have the limit: lim j → ∞ ∫ Ω 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z ) ​ 𝔭 j ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) = ∫ Ω 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z ) ​ 𝔭 ∞ ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) . \lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\mathsf{S}(Z)\,\mathfrak{p}_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z)=\int_{\Omega}\mathsf{S}(Z)\,\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}(\mathrm{d}Z). (B.15) Proof. First, we show that 𝔭 ∞ ∈ 𝒫 1 ​ ( Ω ) \mathfrak{p}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\Omega) . Let δ Z 0 \delta_{Z_{0}} be the Dirac measure at an arbitrary reference point Z 0 ∈ Ω Z_{0}\in\Omega . By the triangle inequality of the Wasserstein-1 metric, we have W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 ∞ , δ Z 0 ) ≤ W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 ∞ , 𝔭 j ) + W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 j , δ Z 0 ) . W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{\infty},\delta_{Z_{0}})\leq W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{\infty},\mathfrak{p}_{j})+W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{j},\delta_{Z_{0}}). (B.16) Since lim j → ∞ W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 j , 𝔭 ∞ ) = 0 \lim_{j\to\infty}W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{j},\mathfrak{p}_{\infty})=0 , the term W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 ∞ , 𝔭 j ) W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{\infty},\mathfrak{p}_{j}) is finite for sufficiently large j j . Additionally, the assumption 𝔭 j ∈ 𝒫 1 ​ ( Ω ) \mathfrak{p}_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\Omega) implies W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 j , δ Z 0 ) = ∫ Ω D Ω ​ ( Z , Z 0 ) ​ 𝔭 j ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) &lt; ∞ W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{j},\delta_{Z_{0}})=\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z_{0})\,\mathfrak{p}_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z)&lt;\infty . Thus, W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 ∞ , δ Z 0 ) &lt; ∞ W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{\infty},\delta_{Z_{0}})&lt;\infty , establishing that 𝔭 ∞ ∈ 𝒫 1 ​ ( Ω ) \mathfrak{p}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\Omega) . Next, we establish the integrability of the scoring rule 𝖲 \mathsf{S} . Since 𝖲 \mathsf{S} is C 𝖲 C_{\mathsf{S}} -Lipschitz continuous, for any Z ∈ Ω Z\in\Omega , we have 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z ) ≤ 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z 0 ) + C 𝖲 ​ D Ω ​ ( Z , Z 0 ) \mathsf{S}(Z)\leq\mathsf{S}(Z_{0})+C_{\mathsf{S}}\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z_{0}) . Integrating both sides with respect to 𝔭 j \mathfrak{p}_{j} yields ∫ Ω 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z ) ​ 𝔭 j ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) ≤ 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z 0 ) + C 𝖲 ​ ∫ Ω D Ω ​ ( Z , Z 0 ) ​ 𝔭 j ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) . \int_{\Omega}\mathsf{S}(Z)\,\mathfrak{p}_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z)\leq\mathsf{S}(Z_{0})+C_{\mathsf{S}}\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z_{0})\,\mathfrak{p}_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z). (B.17) Because 𝔭 j ∈ 𝒫 1 ​ ( Ω ) \mathfrak{p}_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\Omega) , its first moment is finite, meaning the integral on the right-hand side is bounded. Thus, 𝖲 \mathsf{S} is integrable with respect to 𝔭 j \mathfrak{p}_{j} . By the exact same logic, since we just established 𝔭 ∞ ∈ 𝒫 1 ​ ( Ω ) \mathfrak{p}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\Omega) , 𝖲 \mathsf{S} is also integrable with respect to 𝔭 ∞ \mathfrak{p}_{\infty} . Finally, we prove the integral convergence. Since 𝖲 \mathsf{S} is absolutely integrable and C 𝖲 C_{\mathsf{S}} -Lipschitz, we directly apply the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality to the scaled function 𝖲 / C 𝖲 \mathsf{S}/C_{\mathsf{S}} : | ∫ Ω 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z ) ​ 𝔭 j ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) − ∫ Ω 𝖲 ⁡ ( Z ) ​ 𝔭 ∞ ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) | ≤ C 𝖲 ​ W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 j , 𝔭 ∞ ) . \left|\int_{\Omega}\mathsf{S}(Z)\,\mathfrak{p}_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z)-\int_{\Omega}\mathsf{S}(Z)\,\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}(\mathrm{d}Z)\right|\leq C_{\mathsf{S}}W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{j},\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}). (B.18) Taking the limit as j → ∞ j\to\infty , the assumption lim j → ∞ W 1 ​ ( 𝔭 j , 𝔭 ∞ ) = 0 \lim_{j\to\infty}W_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{j},\mathfrak{p}_{\infty})=0 ensures that the right-hand side vanishes. Expressing Z ∈ Ω Z\in\Omega as ( q , v ) (q,v) , the desired convergence result follows immediately. ∎ B.3 Ergodicity of the Dynamical Models and the Augmented Process In Assumption 1 (ii), we assume that the exact augmented state–filter process { Z j } j ∈ ℤ + \{Z_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} is an ergodic Markov chain admitting a unique invariant measure. In this subsection, we outline theoretical foundations that justify this assumption. The first part of this analysis, in B.3.1 , establishes that the underlying physical state process { v j † } j ∈ ℤ + \{v_{j}^{\dagger}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} is ergodic, which serves as a building block in our approach to proving the ergodicity of the joint state–filter process in B.3.2 . B.3.1 Ergodicity of the Physical State Process Recall that our generic dynamical model for the unobserved physical state is given by v j + 1 † = Ψ ⁡ ( v j † ) + ξ j † , j ∈ ℤ + , v_{j+1}^{\dagger}=\Psi(v_{j}^{\dagger})+\xi_{j}^{\dagger},\qquad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}, (B.19) where ξ j † ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) \xi_{j}^{\dagger}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma) are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) system noise vectors and Σ \Sigma is an invertible covariance matrix. This defines a time-homogeneous Markov process on ℝ d v \mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} for every initial distribution of v 0 † v_{0}^{\dagger} . Here we sketch a methodology for establishing ergodicity of the resulting process, drawing on the approach described in Meyn and Tweedie 2012 ; Hairer 2021 . This approach combines an assumed Lyapunov structure induced by the deterministic part of the dynamics, with a probabilistic analysis confined to a compact subset of phase space, using the controllability implied by the noise having invertible covariance matrix. To formalize the probabilistic setup, let q ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) be an initial distribution for the state v 0 † v_{0}^{\dagger} . We denote by ℙ q \mathbb{P}_{q} the probability law of the entire state trajectory (the probability measure on the path) initialized with v 0 † ∼ q v_{0}^{\dagger}\sim q , i.e. ℙ q := Law ⁡ ( { v j † } j ∈ ℤ + ∣ v 0 † ∼ q ) ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ( ℝ d v ) ℤ + ) . \mathbb{P}_{q}:=\Law\left(\{v^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\mid v^{\dagger}_{0}\sim q\right)\in\mathcal{P}\left((\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}})^{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\right). (B.20) If the initial state is a deterministic point, i.e., q = δ v 0 † q=\delta_{v_{0}^{\dagger}} , we write ℙ v 0 † \mathbb{P}_{v_{0}^{\dagger}} . The corresponding expectation operator over these paths is denoted by 𝔼 ℙ q \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}} . Recall 𝖯 \mathsf{P} from ( 2.8 ), the transition kernel of the state process; hence the marginal distribution of v j † v_{j}^{\dagger} is 𝖯 j ​ q \mathsf{P}^{j}q . For the dynamical models considered in this paper, one can prove (see below) that a, possibly weighted, total variation (TV) distance between any two paths decays exponentially fast. Since the weighted TV distances bound the standard unweighted TV distance ‖ ∙ ‖ TV \|\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} we obtain that ‖ 𝖯 n ​ q − 𝖯 n ​ q ′ ‖ TV → 0 \|\mathsf{P}^{n}q-\mathsf{P}^{n}q^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\to 0 with exponential rate as n → ∞ n\to\infty , for any initial probability measures q , q ′ q,q^{\prime} . Furthermore, there exists a unique invariant measure q ∞ q_{\infty} , implying ‖ 𝖯 n ​ q − q ∞ ‖ TV → 0 \|\mathsf{P}^{n}q-q_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\to 0 . Under our strict positivity assumption on covariance Σ \Sigma , q ∞ q_{\infty} is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. Indeed, due to the specific form of the kernel (and in particular the fact that Σ &gt; 0 \Sigma&gt;0 ), 𝖯 ​ q \mathsf{P}q is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure for any probability measure q ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) , so this is true in particular the invariant measure q ∞ = 𝖯 ​ q ∞ q_{\infty}=\mathsf{P}q_{\infty} . Existence and uniqueness, along with exponential convergence, can be established by using the methodology pioneered by Nummelin 2004 . In particular it rests on an application of Harris’s theorem ( Hairer 2021 , Theorem 3.6) ; we sketch the proof in our context. Broadly, it needs to be shown that the dynamics admit a Lyapunov function and that the level sets of this Lyapunov function are so-called “small sets” ( Meyn and Tweedie 2012 ) . A Lyapunov function is a nonnegative function V V so that 𝔼 ⁡ ( V ⁡ ( v j + 1 † ) | v j † = x ) ≤ α ​ V ​ ( x ) + r , with 0 &lt; α &lt; 1 and r &gt; 0 . \mathbb{E}(V(v_{j+1}^{\dagger})|v_{j}^{\dagger}=x)\leq\alpha V(x)+r,\qquad\text{with $0&lt;\alpha&lt;1$ and $r&gt;0$}. (B.21) Taking V ⁡ ( v † ) = ‖ v † ‖ 2 V(v^{\dagger})=\|v^{\dagger}\|^{2} as a Lyapunov function for all the models considered in this paper except the doubling-angle map, which we put aside for the moment, this estimate follows from a priori estimates shown, for instance, in Oljača et al. 2019 for a class of models which includes the Lorenz ’63 (Subsection 4.5 ) and Lorenz ’96 (Subsection 4.6 ); see, for example, Mattingly et al. 2002 ; Law et al. 2016 . For the doubling-angle model (Subsection 4.4 ), a constant Lyapunov function suffices as the entire state space (the compact 1D torus) turns out to be small. A set A A of the state space is small if there is 0 ≤ α &lt; 1 0\leq\alpha&lt;1 so that ‖ 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , x ) − 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , y ) ‖ TV ≤ 2 ​ ( 1 − α ) for all x , y ∈ A . \|\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},x)-\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},y)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq 2(1-\alpha)\qquad\text{for all $x,y\in A$}. (B.22) To prove that the level sets of our Lyapunov function are small sets, we use the Bretagnolle–Huber inequality: ‖ 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , x ) − 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , y ) ‖ TV ≤ 2 ​ [ 1 − 1 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( − 𝒟 KL ​ ( 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , x ) , 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , y ) ) ) ] . \|\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},x)-\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},y)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq 2\big[1-\frac{1}{2}\exp\big(-\mathcal{D}_{\text{KL}}(\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},x),\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},y))\big)\big]. (B.23) For a set to be small it is thus sufficient that the function ( x , y ) ↦ 𝒟 KL ​ ( 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , x ) , 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , y ) ) (x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{\text{KL}}(\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},x),\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},y)) is bounded on that set. Specialising to our setup we obtain, using the formula for the KL divergence between Gaussians ( Bach et al. 2025b , Chapter 11) , 𝒟 KL ​ ( 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , x ) , 𝖯 ⁡ ( ∙ , y ) ) ≤ 1 2 ​ ( Ψ ⁡ ( x ) − Ψ ⁡ ( y ) ) ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ ( Ψ ⁡ ( x ) − Ψ ⁡ ( y ) ) , \mathcal{D}_{\text{KL}}(\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},x),\mathsf{P}(\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet},y))\leq\frac{1}{2}(\Psi(x)-\Psi(y))^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\Psi(x)-\Psi(y)), (B.24) which is clearly bounded on level sets of V V . We stress however that here we use the non-degeneracy of the noise in our models, i.e., the fact that the covariance matrix Σ \Sigma is full-rank. If the noise is degenerate, the statement of Harris’s theorem is no longer true in general without compensating assumptions, and the analysis becomes more complicated – see, for example, the analysis of the Lorenz ’63 model with degenerate noise in Mattingly et al. 2002 . A consequence of the exponential ergodicity ( Revuz 1984 , Ch. 6, Theorem 1.8) is that the algebra of tail events 𝒜 \mathcal{A} , and therefore also the algebra of invariant events, is ℙ q \mathbb{P}_{q} -trivial for every initial probability q q . This means that for any event A ∈ 𝒜 A\in\mathcal{A} , we have either ℙ q ​ ( A ) = 0 \mathbb{P}_{q}(A)=0 for all q q , or ℙ q ​ ( A ) = 1 \mathbb{P}_{q}(A)=1 for all q q . This entails the following generalisation of the ergodic theorem: Let ϕ \phi be a function integrable with respect to the invariant measure q ∞ q_{\infty} . Then the time average satisfies 1 N ​ ∑ j = 1 N ϕ ⁡ ( v j † ) → ∫ ϕ ​ d ​ q ∞ , ℙ q ​ -almost surely for any initial ​ q ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) . \frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\phi(v_{j}^{\dagger})\to\int\phi\,\mathrm{d}q_{\infty},\qquad\mathbb{P}_{q}\text{-almost surely for any initial }q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}). (B.25) To see this, note that the event where the limit superior equals the limit inferior ( lim sup = lim inf \limsup=\liminf ) of the left-hand side of ( B.25 ) is an invariant event. By the ergodic theorem, this event occurs with ℙ q ∞ = 1 \mathbb{P}_{q_{\infty}}=1 . Due to the triviality property, it also occurs with ℙ q = 1 \mathbb{P}_{q}=1 for any initial distribution q q . With regards to the convergence of these averages in L 1 L^{1} with respect to ℙ q \mathbb{P}_{q} for an arbitrary q ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) , we require the condition that the random variables { ϕ ⁡ ( v j † ) } j ≥ 1 \{\phi(v_{j}^{\dagger})\}_{j\geq 1} are uniformly integrable. This uniform integrability is satisfied if initialized at q = q ∞ q=q_{\infty} , since the sequence { v j † } \{v_{j}^{\dagger}\} then consists of identically distributed random variables. B.3.2 Ergodicity of the Joint State–Filter Process We now use the results from B.3.1 , concerning ergodicity of the physical state process { v j † } j ∈ ℤ + \{v_{j}^{\dagger}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} , to establish ergodicity of the joint state–filter augmented process. We consider the settings where the filter is either the true filtering distribution, or some parameterized approximation. We employ the notation established in Subsection 3.2 . To this end, recall the definition of the augmented process Z j = ( q j , v j † ) ∈ Ω = 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) × ℝ d v Z_{j}=(q_{j},v_{j}^{\dagger})\in\Omega=\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}})\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} defined by ( 2.1 ) and ( 3.4 ). The transition from j j to j + 1 j+1 of the process { Z j } j ∈ ℤ + \{Z_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} is determined by the autonomous evolution of the signal and the recursive update of the filter: v j + 1 † \displaystyle v_{j+1}^{\dagger} = Ψ ⁡ ( v j † ) + ξ j † , ξ j † ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) , \displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{\dagger})+\xi_{j}^{\dagger},\quad\xi_{j}^{\dagger}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma), (B.26a) y j + 1 † \displaystyle y_{j+1}^{\dagger} = h ⁡ ( v j + 1 † ) + η j + 1 † , η j + 1 † ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Γ ) , \displaystyle=h(v_{j+1}^{\dagger})+\eta_{j+1}^{\dagger},\quad\eta_{j+1}^{\dagger}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Gamma), (B.26b) q j + 1 \displaystyle q_{j+1} = 𝖡 θ ​ ( 𝖰𝖯 ​ q j , y j + 1 † ) , \displaystyle=\mathsf{B}_{\theta}\bigl(\mathsf{Q}\mathsf{P}q_{j};y_{j+1}^{\dagger}\bigr), (B.26c) If 𝖡 \mathsf{B} is realizable for some choice θ = θ B \theta=\theta_{B} then this formulation also includes the true filter. We make this realizability assumption simply to streamline the analysis that follows to apply to both the true filter and the approximate filter; it is not otherwise needed. We fix the parameter θ \theta and write 𝖥 \mathsf{F} for the map: 𝖥 : 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) × ℝ d y → 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) ; ( q , y ) ↦ 𝖡 θ ​ ( 𝖰𝖯 ​ q , y ) , \mathsf{F}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}})\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}});\quad(q,y)\mapsto\mathsf{B}_{\theta}\bigl(\mathsf{Q}\mathsf{P}q;y\bigr), (B.27) which iterates the approximate filter. Using ( B.26 ), we could include the observation y j + 1 † y_{j+1}^{\dagger} into the augmented state Z j Z_{j} as a third component, but doing so is unnecessary – see ( 3.19 ). To this end we write ( B.26 ) as v j + 1 † \displaystyle v_{j+1}^{\dagger} = Ψ ⁡ ( v j † ) + ξ j † , ξ j † ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) , \displaystyle=\Psi(v_{j}^{\dagger})+\xi_{j}^{\dagger},\quad\xi_{j}^{\dagger}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma), (B.28a) q j + 1 \displaystyle q_{j+1} = 𝖥 ⁡ ( q j , h ⁡ ( v j + 1 † ) + η j + 1 † ) . \displaystyle=\mathsf{F}\bigl(q_{j};h(v_{j+1}^{\dagger})+\eta_{j+1}^{\dagger}\bigr). (B.28b) As in the preceding subsection we assume that Σ \Sigma is an invertible covariance matrix. We establish an ergodicity result for the joint process Z j Z_{j} defined by ( B.28 ), relying on the state ergodicity ( B.3.1 ) and a stability assumption on the filter dynamics. As discussed, given an initial probability measure q ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) , equations ( B.26a ) and ( B.26b ) define the path distribution ℙ q \mathbb{P}_{q} for the state–observation trajectory. Likewise, given μ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mu\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega) , the full model ( B.26a )–( B.26c ) defines a trajectory distribution ℙ μ , Ω \mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega} for { Z j } j ∈ ℤ + \{Z_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} , i.e., ℙ μ , Ω := Law ⁡ ( { Z j } j ∈ ℤ + ∣ Z 0 ∼ μ ) ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ( Ω ) ℤ + ) . \mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}:=\Law\left(\{Z_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\mid Z_{0}\sim\mu\right)\in\mathcal{P}\left((\Omega)^{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\right). (B.29) The marginal distribution of the physical state process under ℙ μ , Ω \mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega} is ℙ q \mathbb{P}_{q} , where q q is the projection of μ \mu onto the state space. Assumption 2 (Filter Stability) . Let { y j † } j ≥ 1 \{y_{j}^{\dagger}\}_{j\geq 1} be a sequence of observations generated under the path measure ℙ q ⋆ \mathbb{P}_{q_{\star}} . Given q 0 , q 0 ′ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) q_{0},q^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) define two sequences of filtering distributions by setting, for j ∈ ℤ + j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+} , q j + 1 \displaystyle q_{j+1} = 𝖥 ⁡ ( q j , y j + 1 † ) , \displaystyle=\mathsf{F}(q_{j},y_{j+1}^{\dagger}), q j + 1 ′ \displaystyle q^{\prime}_{j+1} = 𝖥 ⁡ ( q j ′ , y j + 1 † ) . \displaystyle=\mathsf{F}(q^{\prime}_{j},y_{j+1}^{\dagger}). Then there exists a metric d d on 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) , and an initial physical state distribution q ⋆ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) q_{\star}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) , such that for any two initial filter conditions q 0 , q 0 ′ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) q_{0},q^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) , we have d ⁡ ( q j , q j ′ ) → 0 ℙ q ⋆ -almost surely as ​ j → ∞ . d(q_{j},q^{\prime}_{j})\to 0\qquad\text{$\mathbb{P}_{q_{\star}}$-almost surely as }j\to\infty. (B.30) Stability of nonlinear filters with respect to misspecified initial distribution is a vast field, see Chigansky et al. 2011 for an overview and further references. Establishing stability, in the sense of Assumption 2 , for the approximate filters considered here, remains to be investigated further. To measure convergence on the augmented space Ω \Omega , we use the bounded metric D ^ Ω ​ ( ( q , v ) , ( q ′ , v ′ ) ) := 𝟏 v ≠ v ′ + min ⁡ { d ⁡ ( q , q ′ ) , 1 } . \widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}\bigl((q,v),(q^{\prime},v^{\prime})\bigr):=\mathbf{1}_{v\neq v^{\prime}}+\min\{d(q,q^{\prime}),1\}. (B.31) Remark 22 (Regarding the metric D ^ Ω \widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega} and physical marginals) . The term 𝟏 v ≠ v ′ \mathbf{1}_{v\neq v^{\prime}} in Equation ( B.31 ) is the trivial metric, defined as 0 0 if v = v ′ v=v^{\prime} and 1 1 otherwise. Moreover, the Wasserstein metric evaluated with respect to the trivial metric coincides with the total variation distance. Therefore, if μ \mu and μ ′ \mu^{\prime} are joint distributions on Ω \Omega , and if q q and q ′ q^{\prime} denote their respective marginals on the physical state space, then convergence of the joint process in W D ^ Ω W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}} implies convergence of the physical marginals in total variation. More precisely, ‖ ℙ q ​ ( v j † ∈ ∙ ) − ℙ q ′ ​ ( v j † ∈ ∙ ) ‖ TV ≤ W D ^ Ω ​ ( ℙ μ , Ω ​ ( Z j ∈ ∙ ) , ℙ μ ′ , Ω ​ ( Z j ∈ ∙ ) ) . \|\mathbb{P}_{q}(v_{j}^{\dagger}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})-\mathbb{P}_{q^{\prime}}(v_{j}^{\dagger}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}}\Bigl(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}(Z_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}),\mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime},\Omega}(Z_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\Bigr). (B.32) Theorem 23 . Suppose that Assumption 2 holds, and that there exists an invariant probability measure q ∞ q_{\infty} for the state process { v j † } j ∈ ℤ + \{v_{j}^{\dagger}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} such that ‖ 𝖯 j ​ q − q ∞ ‖ TV → 0 \|\mathsf{P}^{j}q-q_{\infty}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\to 0 as j → ∞ j\to\infty for any probability q ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) . Then, for any two initial joint probability measures μ , μ ′ \mu,\mu^{\prime} on Ω \Omega , we have W D ^ Ω ​ ( ℙ μ , Ω ​ ( Z j ∈ ∙ ) , ℙ μ ′ , Ω ​ ( Z j ∈ ∙ ) ) → 0 as ​ j → ∞ , W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}}\Bigl(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}(Z_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}),\mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime},\Omega}(Z_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\Bigr)\to 0\qquad\text{as }j\to\infty, (B.33) where W D ^ Ω W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}} is the Wasserstein metric induced by the distance D ^ Ω \widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega} . Furthermore, there exists a unique invariant probability measure μ ∞ \mu_{\infty} for the augmented state process { Z j } j ∈ ℤ + \{Z_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} . Ergodicity of the joint state–filter augmented process has been demonstrated for instance in Budhiraja 2003 , albeit only for the exact filter (and under different conditions which ensure stability of the exact but not necessarily any approximating filter). Furthermore, the method of proof differs from the one we in introduce here. We prove Theorem 23 using the theory of coupling, the basics of which we now outline. Let { U j } j ∈ ℤ + \{U_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} and { U j ′ } j ∈ ℤ + \{U^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} be two stochastic processes on the same state space defined on probability spaces ( Ω U , 𝒜 U , ℙ U ) (\Omega_{U},\mathcal{A}_{U},\mathbb{P}_{U}) and ( Ω U ′ , 𝒜 U ′ , ℙ U ′ ) (\Omega^{\prime}_{U},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{U},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{U}) . A coupling of U , U ′ U,U^{\prime} is a pair process { ( U ^ j , U ^ j ′ ) } j ∈ ℤ + \{(\widehat{U}_{j},\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} on a joint probability space ( Ω ^ , 𝒜 ^ , 𝕄 ) (\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\mathbb{M}) such that Law ⁡ ( U ^ ) = Law ⁡ ( U ) \Law(\widehat{U})=\Law(U) and Law ⁡ ( U ^ ′ ) = Law ⁡ ( U ′ ) \Law(\widehat{U}^{\prime})=\Law(U^{\prime}) . For such a coupling, consider the coupling time T := min ⁡ { j ∈ ℤ + ∣ U ^ k = U ^ k ′ ​ for all ​ k ≥ j } . T:=\min\bigl\{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\mid\widehat{U}_{k}=\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{k}\text{ for all }k\geq j\bigr\}. (B.34) Here T ∈ ℤ + T\in\mathbb{Z}_{+} is a random variable; we call the coupling successful if T &lt; ∞ T&lt;\infty almost surely. A fundamental fact of coupling theory ( Lindvall 2002 ) states that for any coupling, ‖ ℙ U ​ ( U j ∈ ∙ ) − ℙ U ′ ​ ( U j ′ ∈ ∙ ) ‖ TV ≤ 2 ​ 𝕄 ​ ( T &gt; j ) . \|\mathbb{P}_{U}(U_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})-\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{U}(U^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq 2\mathbb{M}(T&gt;j). (B.35) Furthermore, there exists a coupling that gives equality in this relation. As a corollary, the total variation distance goes to zero if and only if there exists a successful coupling between U U and U ′ U^{\prime} . of Theorem 23 . The existence of μ ∞ \mu_{\infty} follows from a Krylov–Bogoliubov argument. The uniqueness is a consequence of the stability result ( B.33 ) which we now prove. Let μ , μ ′ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega) . It is sufficient to prove the stability result ( B.33 ) when μ \mu has the marginal state distribution q ⋆ q_{\star} , the probability that appears in Assumption 2 , and μ ′ \mu^{\prime} is arbitrary. The case of two arbitrary initial distributions follows via the triangle inequality. Write q = q ⋆ q=q_{\star} and q ′ q^{\prime} for the marginals of μ \mu and μ ′ \mu^{\prime} on the physical state space ℝ d v \mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} . The distributions of the state trajectories under ℙ μ , Ω \mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega} and ℙ μ ′ , Ω \mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime},\Omega} are given by the path measures ℙ q \mathbb{P}_{q} and ℙ q ′ \mathbb{P}_{q^{\prime}} . Since the state process is ergodic, we have ‖ ℙ q ​ ( v j † ∈ ∙ ) − ℙ q ′ ​ ( v j † ∈ ∙ ) ‖ TV = ‖ 𝖯 j ​ q − 𝖯 j ​ q ′ ‖ TV → 0 , as ​ j → ∞ . \|\mathbb{P}_{q}(v_{j}^{\dagger}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})-\mathbb{P}_{q^{\prime}}(v_{j}^{\dagger}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\|\mathsf{P}^{j}q-\mathsf{P}^{j}q^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\to 0,\qquad\text{ as }j\to\infty. (B.36) We conclude from ( B.36 ) and the coupling result that there exists a coupling { ( U ^ j , U ^ j ′ ) } \{(\widehat{U}_{j},\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{j})\} on some space ( Ω ^ , 𝒜 ^ , 𝕄 ) (\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\mathbb{M}) such that Law ⁡ ( ( U ^ j ) j ∈ ℤ + ) = ℙ q , Law ⁡ ( ( U ^ j ′ ) j ∈ ℤ + ) = ℙ q ′ , \Law\bigl((\widehat{U}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{q},\qquad\Law\bigl((\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{q^{\prime}}, (B.37) and 𝕄 ⁡ ( U ^ k = U ^ k ′ ​ for all ​ k ≥ j ) = 𝕄 ⁡ ( T ≤ j ) → 1 as ​ j → ∞ . \mathbb{M}\left(\widehat{U}_{k}=\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{k}\text{ for all }k\geq j\right)=\mathbb{M}(T\leq j)\to 1\qquad\text{as }j\to\infty. (B.38) We now extend this state coupling to a coupling of the augmented processes. Let { η j † } j ∈ ℤ + \{\eta_{j}^{\dagger}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} be an i.i.d. sequence with distribution 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Γ ) \mathsf{N}(0,\Gamma) , independent of the coupled state trajectories ( U ^ , U ^ ′ ) (\widehat{U},\widehat{U}^{\prime}) . We define y ^ j := h ⁡ ( U ^ j ) + η j † , y ^ j ′ := h ⁡ ( U ^ j ′ ) + η j † . \widehat{y}_{j}:=h(\widehat{U}_{j})+\eta_{j}^{\dagger},\qquad\widehat{y}^{\prime}_{j}:=h(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{j})+\eta_{j}^{\dagger}. (B.39) Since U ^ \widehat{U} has path law ℙ q ⋆ \mathbb{P}_{q_{\star}} and the noise sequence { η j † } \{\eta_{j}^{\dagger}\} is independent with the correct distribution, the pair ( U ^ , y ^ ) (\widehat{U},\widehat{y}) has the same state–observation law as the model initialized from q ⋆ q_{\star} . Similarly, ( U ^ ′ , y ^ ′ ) (\widehat{U}^{\prime},\widehat{y}^{\prime}) has the correct state–observation law initialized from q ′ q^{\prime} . Choose q ^ 0 \widehat{q}_{0} and q ^ 0 ′ \widehat{q}^{\prime}_{0} such that Law ⁡ ( q ^ 0 , U ^ 0 ) = μ , Law ⁡ ( q ^ 0 ′ , U ^ 0 ′ ) = μ ′ . \Law(\widehat{q}_{0},\widehat{U}_{0})=\mu,\qquad\Law(\widehat{q}^{\prime}_{0},\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{0})=\mu^{\prime}. (B.40) Then define q ^ j + 1 = 𝖥 ⁡ ( q ^ j , y ^ j + 1 ) , q ^ j + 1 ′ = 𝖥 ⁡ ( q ^ j ′ , y ^ j + 1 ′ ) . \widehat{q}_{j+1}=\mathsf{F}(\widehat{q}_{j},\widehat{y}_{j+1}),\qquad\widehat{q}^{\prime}_{j+1}=\mathsf{F}(\widehat{q}^{\prime}_{j},\widehat{y}^{\prime}_{j+1}). (B.41) where 𝖥 \mathsf{F} is the map defined in Equation ( B.27 ) which iterates the approximate filter. The resulting processes Z ^ j = ( q ^ j , U ^ j ) , Z ^ j ′ = ( q ^ j ′ , U ^ j ′ ) \widehat{Z}_{j}=(\widehat{q}_{j},\widehat{U}_{j}),\qquad\widehat{Z}^{\prime}_{j}=(\widehat{q}^{\prime}_{j},\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{j}) (B.42) form a valid coupling of the augmented processes with laws ℙ μ , Ω \mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega} and ℙ μ ′ , Ω \mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime},\Omega} . Evaluating the Wasserstein metric W D ^ Ω W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}} using this coupling, and letting 𝔼 𝕄 \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{M}} denote the expectation with respect to 𝕄 \mathbb{M} , we obtain W D ^ Ω ​ ( ℙ μ , Ω ​ ( Z j ∈ ∙ ) , ℙ μ ′ , Ω ​ ( Z j ∈ ∙ ) ) \displaystyle W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}}\Bigl(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}(Z_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}),\mathbb{P}_{\mu^{\prime},\Omega}(Z_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\Bigr) (B.43) ≤ 𝔼 𝕄 ​ [ D ^ Ω ​ ( Z ^ j , Z ^ j ′ ) ] \displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{M}}\bigl[\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}(\widehat{Z}_{j},\widehat{Z}^{\prime}_{j})\bigr] = 𝕄 ⁡ ( U ^ j ≠ U ^ j ′ ) + 𝔼 𝕄 ​ [ min ⁡ { d ⁡ ( q ^ j , q ^ j ′ ) , 1 } ] . \displaystyle=\mathbb{M}(\widehat{U}_{j}\neq\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{j})+\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{M}}\bigl[\min\{d(\widehat{q}_{j},\widehat{q}^{\prime}_{j}),1\}\bigr]. The first term goes to zero since 𝕄 ⁡ ( U ^ j ≠ U ^ j ′ ) ≤ 𝕄 ⁡ ( T &gt; j ) → 0 \mathbb{M}(\widehat{U}_{j}\neq\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{j})\leq\mathbb{M}(T&gt;j)\to 0 . Because the state coupling is successful, there exists an almost surely finite time T T such that U ^ k = U ^ k ′ \widehat{U}_{k}=\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{k} for all k ≥ T k\geq T . By the construction with the same observational noise, this implies y ^ k = y ^ k ′ for all ​ k ≥ T . \widehat{y}_{k}=\widehat{y}^{\prime}_{k}\qquad\text{for all }k\geq T. (B.44) Hence, from time T T onward, the two filter recursions are driven by the same sequence of observations. This tail is generated by the reference trajectory ( U ^ , y ^ ) (\widehat{U},\widehat{y}) , whose law is the state–observation law under ℙ q ⋆ \mathbb{P}_{q_{\star}} . Therefore, applying Assumption 2 from the finite time T T onward gives d ⁡ ( q ^ j , q ^ j ′ ) → 0 𝕄 ​ -almost surely . d(\widehat{q}_{j},\widehat{q}^{\prime}_{j})\to 0\qquad\mathbb{M}\text{-almost surely}. (B.45) Since min ⁡ { d ⁡ ( q ^ j , q ^ j ′ ) , 1 } \min\{d(\widehat{q}_{j},\widehat{q}^{\prime}_{j}),1\} is bounded by 1 1 , the dominated convergence theorem guarantees that the second term in ( B.43 ) converges to zero. ∎ Corollary 24 (Convergence of marginal laws) . Let the assumptions of Theorem 23 hold, and let μ ∞ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mu_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega) denote the unique invariant measure of the augmented process. For any initial law Z 0 ∼ μ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( Ω ) Z_{0}\sim\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega) , consider μ j = Law ( Z j ) = ℙ μ , Ω ( Z j ∈ ⋅ ) , j ∈ ℤ + , \mu_{j}=\Law(Z_{j})=\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}(Z_{j}\in\cdot),\qquad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}, (B.46) then we have W D ^ Ω ​ ( μ j , μ ∞ ) → 0 as ​ j → ∞ . W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}}(\mu_{j},\mu_{\infty})\to 0\qquad\text{as }j\to\infty. (B.47) Proof. Apply Theorem 23 with μ ′ = μ ∞ \mu^{\prime}=\mu_{\infty} . Since μ ∞ \mu_{\infty} is invariant, we have Law ℙ μ ∞ , Ω ⁡ ( Z j ) = μ ∞ for all ​ j ∈ ℤ + . \Law_{\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\infty},\Omega}}(Z_{j})=\mu_{\infty}\qquad\text{for all }j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}. (B.48) Therefore, W D ^ Ω ​ ( μ j , μ ∞ ) = W D ^ Ω ​ ( ℙ μ , Ω ​ ( Z j ∈ ∙ ) , ℙ μ ∞ , Ω ​ ( Z j ∈ ∙ ) ) → 0 , W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}}(\mu_{j},\mu_{\infty})=W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}}\Bigl(\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}(Z_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet}),\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\infty},\Omega}(Z_{j}\in\mathord{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.67,0.67}\bullet})\Bigr)\to 0, (B.49) which proves the claim. ∎ Theorem 23 provides the existence of the invariant measure and convergence to it in the Wasserstein distance induced by the bounded metric D ^ Ω \widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega} . However, the convergence statement in Assumption 1 (ii) is formulated using the Wasserstein distance induced by the unbounded metric D Ω ​ ( ( q , v ) , ( q ′ , v ′ ) ) = W 1 ​ ( q , q ′ ) + ‖ v − v ′ ‖ . \mathrm{D}_{\Omega}\bigl((q,v),(q^{\prime},v^{\prime})\bigr)=W_{1}(q,q^{\prime})+\|v-v^{\prime}\|. (B.50) We now explain how the convergence in W D ^ Ω W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}} can be upgraded to convergence in W D Ω W_{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}} once the augmented process has the required first-moment control. The key point is that D ^ Ω \widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega} controls the bounded truncation min ⁡ { D Ω , 1 } \min\{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega},1\} , while uniform integrability controls the tails of the unbounded cost D Ω \mathrm{D}_{\Omega} . Crucially, while Theorem 23 and Corollary 24 apply to any generic filter recursion satisfying the stability Assumption 2 , establishing the necessary uniform integrability for the unbounded metric requires specific structural properties. Therefore, for the remainder of this subsection, we restrict our focus to the exact augmented process , where the filter is the true Bayesian posterior q j = π j := Law ⁡ ( v j † ∣ Y j † ) q_{j}=\pi_{j}:=\Law\bigl(v_{j}^{\dagger}\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}\bigr) . Lemma 25 (Moment control for the exact augmented process) . Let Z j = ( π j , v j † ) Z_{j}=(\pi_{j},v_{j}^{\dagger}) be the exact augmented process. Let Z 0 ∼ μ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( Ω ) Z_{0}\sim\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega) , and let q ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( ℝ d v ) q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}) be the projection of μ \mu onto the physical state space. Assume that the exact filter is initialized consistently with the state–observation path law under ℙ μ , Ω \mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega} , so that π j = ℙ μ , Ω ( v j † ∈ ⋅ ∣ Y j † ) \pi_{j}=\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}(v_{j}^{\dagger}\in\cdot\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}) for every j ∈ ℤ + j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+} . Suppose that the physical state process satisfies the quadratic Lyapunov estimate ( B.21 ), that is, ( B.21 ) holds with V ⁡ ( v † ) = ‖ v † ‖ 2 V(v^{\dagger})=\|v^{\dagger}\|^{2} . If 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ‖ v 0 † ‖ 2 &lt; ∞ \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{0}^{\dagger}\|^{2}&lt;\infty , then sup j ≥ 0 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ‖ v j † ‖ 2 &lt; ∞ \sup_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|^{2}&lt;\infty . Consequently, the family { ‖ v j † ‖ } j ≥ 0 \{\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|\}_{j\geq 0} is uniformly integrable under ℙ q \mathbb{P}_{q} : lim R → ∞ sup j ≥ 0 𝔼 ℙ q [ ∥ v j † ∥ 𝟏 { ∥ v j † ∥ &gt; R } ] = 0 . \lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\left[\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|\mathbf{1}_{\{\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|&gt;R\}}\right]=0. (B.51) Moreover, for Z ⋆ = ( δ 0 , 0 ) Z_{\star}=(\delta_{0},0) , the family { D Ω ​ ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) } j ≥ 0 \{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})\}_{j\geq 0} is uniformly integrable under ℙ μ , Ω \mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega} : lim R → ∞ sup j ≥ 0 𝔼 ℙ μ , Ω [ D Ω ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) 𝟏 { D Ω ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) &gt; R } ] = 0 . \lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}}\left[\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})&gt;R\}}\right]=0. (B.52) Proof. The physical state marginal of ℙ μ , Ω \mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega} is ℙ q \mathbb{P}_{q} . By the quadratic Lyapunov estimate and the tower property, 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ‖ v j + 1 † ‖ 2 = 𝔼 ℙ q ​ [ 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ( ‖ v j + 1 † ‖ 2 ∣ v j † ) ] ≤ α ​ 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ‖ v j † ‖ 2 + r . \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{j+1}^{\dagger}\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\left[\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\left(\|v_{j+1}^{\dagger}\|^{2}\mid v_{j}^{\dagger}\right)\right]\leq\alpha\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|^{2}+r. (B.53) Iterating this inequality yields 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ‖ v j † ‖ 2 ≤ α j ​ 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ‖ v 0 † ‖ 2 + r ⁡ ( 1 − α j ) 1 − α ≤ 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ‖ v 0 † ‖ 2 + r 1 − α . \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|^{2}\leq\alpha^{j}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{0}^{\dagger}\|^{2}+\frac{r(1-\alpha^{j})}{1-\alpha}\leq\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{0}^{\dagger}\|^{2}+\frac{r}{1-\alpha}. (B.54) Hence sup j ≥ 0 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ‖ v j † ‖ 2 &lt; ∞ \sup_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|^{2}&lt;\infty . This implies uniform integrability of { ‖ v j † ‖ } j ≥ 0 \{\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|\}_{j\geq 0} , since sup j ≥ 0 𝔼 ℙ q [ ∥ v j † ∥ 𝟏 { ∥ v j † ∥ &gt; R } ] ≤ 1 R sup j ≥ 0 𝔼 ℙ q ∥ v j † ∥ 2 → 0 , as R → ∞ . \sup_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\left[\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|\mathbf{1}_{\{\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|&gt;R\}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{R}\sup_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|^{2}\to 0,\text{ as }R\to\infty. (B.55) It remains to pass from the physical state to the exact augmented state. Since π j = ℙ μ , Ω ( v j † ∈ ⋅ ∣ Y j † ) \pi_{j}=\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}(v_{j}^{\dagger}\in\cdot\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}) , we have D Ω ​ ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) = W 1 ​ ( π j , δ 0 ) + | v j † | = 𝔼 ℙ μ , Ω ​ [ ‖ v j † ‖ ∣ Y j † ] + ‖ v j † ‖ . \mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})=W_{1}(\pi_{j},\delta_{0})+\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}}\left[\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}\right]+\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|. (B.56) By Jensen’s inequality and the identity of the physical marginals, 𝔼 ℙ μ , Ω ​ [ D Ω ​ ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) 2 ] ≤ 2 ​ 𝔼 ℙ μ , Ω ​ [ ( 𝔼 ℙ μ , Ω ​ [ ‖ v j † ‖ ∣ Y j † ] ) 2 ] + 2 ​ 𝔼 ℙ μ , Ω ​ ‖ v j † ‖ 2 ≤ 4 ​ 𝔼 ℙ q ​ ‖ v j † ‖ 2 . \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}}\left[\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})^{2}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}}\left[\left(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}}\left[\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}\right]\right)^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}}\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|^{2}\leq 4\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{q}}\|v_{j}^{\dagger}\|^{2}. (B.57) Therefore, sup j ≥ 0 𝔼 ℙ μ , Ω ​ [ D Ω ​ ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) 2 ] &lt; ∞ . \sup_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}}\left[\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})^{2}\right]&lt;\infty. This second-moment bound implies uniform integrability of { D Ω ​ ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) } j ≥ 0 \{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})\}_{j\geq 0} , since sup j ≥ 0 𝔼 ℙ μ , Ω [ D Ω ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) 𝟏 { D Ω ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) &gt; R } ] ≤ 1 R sup j ≥ 0 𝔼 ℙ μ , Ω [ D Ω ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) 2 ] → 0 , as R → ∞ . \sup_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}}\left[\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})&gt;R\}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{R}\sup_{j\geq 0}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\mu,\Omega}}\left[\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})^{2}\right]\to 0,\text{ as }R\to\infty. (B.58) ∎ Lemma 26 (From D ^ Ω \widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega} to D Ω \mathrm{D}_{\Omega} ) . Consider the filter metric in D ^ Ω \widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega} with d = W 1 d=W_{1} . (B.59) Proof. Let r ⁡ ( Z ) = D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ⋆ ) r(Z)=\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z_{\star}) .
