(B.61) We first show that μ ∞ \mu_{\infty} has finite first moment with respect to D Ω \mathrm{D}_{\Omega} . For every M &gt; 0 M&gt;0 , the truncated function min ⁡ { r ⁡ ( ⋅ ) , M } \min\{r(\cdot),M\} is bounded and Lipschitz with respect to the bounded metric min ⁡ { D Ω , 1 } \min\{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega},1\} . Therefore, by ( B.61 ), ∫ Ω min ⁡ { r ⁡ ( Z ) , M } ​ μ ∞ ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) = lim j → ∞ ∫ Ω min ⁡ { r ⁡ ( Z ) , M } ​ μ j ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) . \int_{\Omega}\min\{r(Z),M\}\mu_{\infty}(\mathrm{d}Z)=\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\min\{r(Z),M\}\mu_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z). (B.62) The uniform integrability assumption on { D Ω ​ ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) } j ≥ 0 \left\{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})\right\}_{j\geq 0} implies sup j ≥ 0 ∫ Ω r ⁡ ( Z ) ​ μ j ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) &lt; ∞ . \sup_{j\geq 0}\int_{\Omega}r(Z)\mu_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z)&lt;\infty. Hence, letting M → ∞ M\to\infty and applying the monotone convergence theorem gives ∫ Ω r ⁡ ( Z ) ​ μ ∞ ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) = lim M → ∞ ∫ Ω ( r ⁡ ( Z ) ∧ M ) ​ μ ∞ ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) ≤ sup j ≥ 0 ∫ Ω r ⁡ ( Z ) ​ μ j ​ ( 𝑑 Z ) &lt; ∞ . \int_{\Omega}r(Z)\mu_{\infty}(\mathrm{d}Z)=\lim_{M\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}(r(Z)\wedge M)\mu_{\infty}(\mathrm{d}Z)\leq\sup_{j\geq 0}\int_{\Omega}r(Z)\mu_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z)&lt;\infty. (B.63) For each j j , choose a coupling γ j ∈ Π ⁡ ( μ j , μ ∞ ) \gamma_{j}\in\Pi(\mu_{j},\mu_{\infty}) such that ∫ Ω × Ω D ^ Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) ​ γ j ​ ( 𝑑 Z , d ​ Z ′ ) ≤ W D ^ Ω ​ ( μ j , μ ∞ ) + 1 j + 1 . \int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})\gamma_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z,\mathrm{d}Z^{\prime})\leq W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}}(\mu_{j},\mu_{\infty})+\frac{1}{j+1}. (B.64) By ( B.60 ) and ( B.64 ), ∫ Ω × Ω min ⁡ { D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) , 1 } ​ γ j ​ ( 𝑑 Z , d ​ Z ′ ) → 0 . \int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\min\{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime}),1\}\gamma_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z,\mathrm{d}Z^{\prime})\to 0. (B.65) Thus D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) → 0 \mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})\to 0 in γ j \gamma_{j} -probability. It remains to upgrade this convergence in probability to convergence in expectation. By the triangle inequality, D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) ≤ r ⁡ ( Z ) + r ⁡ ( Z ′ ) \mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})\leq r(Z)+r(Z^{\prime}) Therefore, for every R &gt; 0 R&gt;0 , D Ω ( Z , Z ′ ) 𝟏 { D Ω ( Z , Z ′ ) &gt; R } ≤ 2 r ( Z ) 𝟏 { r ( Z ) &gt; R / 2 } + 2 r ( Z ′ ) 𝟏 { r ( Z ′ ) &gt; R / 2 } . \mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})&gt;R\}}\leq 2r(Z)\mathbf{1}_{\{r(Z)&gt;R/2\}}+2r(Z^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{\{r(Z^{\prime})&gt;R/2\}}. (B.66) Integrating this inequality with respect to γ j \gamma_{j} gives ∫ Ω × Ω D Ω ( Z , Z ′ ) 𝟏 { D Ω ( Z , Z ′ ) &gt; R } γ j ( d Z , d Z ′ ) ≤ 2 ∫ Ω r ( Z ) 𝟏 { r ( Z ) &gt; R / 2 } μ j ( d Z ) + 2 ∫ Ω r ( Z ) 𝟏 { r ( Z ) &gt; R / 2 } μ ∞ ( d Z ) . \int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})&gt;R\}}\gamma_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z,\mathrm{d}Z^{\prime})\leq 2\int_{\Omega}r(Z)\mathbf{1}_{\{r(Z)&gt;R/2\}}\mu_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z)\\ +2\int_{\Omega}r(Z)\mathbf{1}_{\{r(Z)&gt;R/2\}}\mu_{\infty}(\mathrm{d}Z). (B.67) The first term on the right-hand side vanishes uniformly in j j as R → ∞ R\to\infty by the assumed uniform integrability. The second term vanishes as R → ∞ R\to\infty by ( B.63 ). Hence the family { D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) } j ≥ 0 \{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})\}_{j\geq 0} under the couplings γ j \gamma_{j} is uniformly integrable. Since D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) → 0 \mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})\to 0 in γ j \gamma_{j} -probability and the family is uniformly integrable, we obtain ∫ Ω × Ω D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) ​ γ j ​ ( 𝑑 Z , d ​ Z ′ ) → 0 . \int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})\gamma_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z,\mathrm{d}Z^{\prime})\to 0. (B.68) Finally, since γ j \gamma_{j} is a coupling of μ j \mu_{j} and μ ∞ \mu_{\infty} , W D Ω ​ ( μ j , μ ∞ ) ≤ ∫ Ω × Ω D Ω ​ ( Z , Z ′ ) ​ γ j ​ ( 𝑑 Z , d ​ Z ′ ) → 0 . W_{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}}(\mu_{j},\mu_{\infty})\leq\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z,Z^{\prime})\gamma_{j}(\mathrm{d}Z,\mathrm{d}Z^{\prime})\to 0. (B.69) This proves the implication. ∎ By synthesizing the bounded metric convergence with the uniform integrability established above, we can now state the main ergodicity result for the exact augmented process, which serves as the rigorous theoretical foundation forAssumption 1 (ii). Theorem 27 (Ergodicity of the exact augmented process in unbounded metric) . Suppose that the physical state { v j † } j ∈ ℤ + \{v_{j}^{\dagger}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}} admits a unique invariant measure q ∞ q_{\infty} (as in Theorem 23 ) and satisfies the Lyapunov estimate ( B.21 ). Furthermore, suppose the filter sequence π j = ℙ ⁡ ( v j † ∣ Y j † ) \pi_{j}=\mathbb{P}(v_{j}^{\dagger}\mid Y_{j}^{\dagger}) satisfies the stability condition in Assumption 2 with d = W 1 d=W_{1} . Let μ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mu\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega) be an initial distribution for the augmented state Z 0 = ( π 0 , v 0 † ) Z_{0}=(\pi_{0},v_{0}^{\dagger}) such that the physical state marginal has a finite second moment (i.e., 𝔼 ​ ‖ v 0 † ‖ 2 &lt; ∞ \mathbb{E}\|v_{0}^{\dagger}\|^{2}&lt;\infty ). Then the augmented process Z j = ( π j , v j † ) Z_{j}=(\pi_{j},v_{j}^{\dagger}) admits a unique invariant probability measure μ ∞ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mu_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega) , which has a finite first moment with respect to D Ω \mathrm{D}_{\Omega} . Furthermore, the marginal laws μ j = Law ⁡ ( Z j ) \mu_{j}=\Law(Z_{j}) converge to μ ∞ \mu_{\infty} in the unbounded Wasserstein metric: lim j → ∞ W D Ω ​ ( μ j , μ ∞ ) = 0 . \lim_{j\to\infty}W_{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}}(\mu_{j},\mu_{\infty})=0. (B.70) Proof. By Theorem 23 and Corollary 24 , there exists a unique invariant measure μ ∞ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( Ω ) \mu_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega) , and the marginal laws satisfy lim j → ∞ W D ^ Ω ​ ( μ j , μ ∞ ) = 0 \lim_{j\to\infty}W_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega}}(\mu_{j},\mu_{\infty})=0 . For the exact augmented process, Lemma 25 guarantees that the Lyapunov condition on the physical state translates to the uniform integrability of the sequence { D Ω ​ ( Z j , Z ⋆ ) } j ≥ 0 \{\mathrm{D}_{\Omega}(Z_{j},Z_{\star})\}_{j\geq 0} . Finally, applying Lemma 26 , this uniform integrability rigorously upgrades the convergence in the bounded metric D ^ Ω \widehat{\mathrm{D}}_{\Omega} to convergence in the unbounded metric D Ω \mathrm{D}_{\Omega} , completing the proof. ∎ Remark 28 (Verification for specific dynamical models) . Theorem 27 verifies Assumption 1 (ii) for the models considered in this paper under the stated non-degeneracy and filter-stability assumptions. Assuming that the underlying system noise is non-degenerate and the exact filter is stable with respect to W 1 W_{1} : • Doubling-angle model (Subsection 4.4 : The physical state space is the 1D torus, which is intrinsically compact. Consequently, the exact filter components π j \pi_{j} are probability measures supported on this compact space. Both ‖ v j † ‖ \|v_{j}^{\dagger}\| and W 1 ​ ( π j , δ 0 ) W_{1}(\pi_{j},\delta_{0}) are uniformly bounded, meaning the uniform integrability requirement is trivially satisfied without any moment assumptions. Therefore, Theorem 27 applies immediately. • Lorenz ’63 and Lorenz ’96 models (Subsections 4.5 and 4.6 : While their state spaces are unbounded Euclidean spaces, the underlying continuous-time ordinary differential equations for both models are strictly dissipative; see, for example, Mattingly et al. 2002 ; Law et al. 2016 . This inherent macroscopic dissipativity naturally translates to the discrete-time Lyapunov estimate ( B.21 ). Thus, as long as the initial state has a finite second moment, uniform second-moment control holds, and Theorem 27 applies. B.4 Flatness of the Rank Histogram of the Filtering Distribution This subsection provides the theoretical justification for the rank-histogram diagnostic and the RHVar \operatorname{RHVar} metric introduced in Subsection 4.2 : Proposition 29 shows that an ensemble sampled from the true filtering distribution has a uniform rank statistic, so a flat rank histogram is the ideal calibration behavior. Proposition 29 . The rank statistic of f ⁡ ( v j † ) f(v^{\dagger}_{j}) with respect to an ensemble { f ⁡ ( v j ( n ) ) } n = 1 N \{f(v^{(n)}_{j})\}_{n=1}^{N} , where { v j ( n ) } n = 1 N \{v^{(n)}_{j}\}_{n=1}^{N} are drawn from the true filtering distribution ℙ ⁡ ( v j † | Y j † ) \mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}) (Definition 1 ) and f f is a scalar function, is uniform over the integers [ 0 , N ] [0,N] . Proof. The proof is a direct application of Theorem 1 in Talts et al. 2020 , which states that for any joint distribution ℙ ⁡ ( θ , z ) \mathbb{P}(\theta,z) , letting ( θ ~ , z ~ ) ∼ ℙ ⁡ ( θ , z ) (\tilde{\theta},\tilde{z})\sim\mathbb{P}(\theta,z) , and { θ ( 1 ) , … , θ ( N ) } ∼ ℙ ⁡ ( θ | z ~ ) \{\theta^{(1)},\ldots,\theta^{(N)}\}\sim\mathbb{P}(\theta\,|\,\tilde{z}) , implies that the rank statistic of f ⁡ ( θ ~ ) f(\tilde{\theta}) with respect to { f ⁡ ( θ ( 1 ) ) , … , f ⁡ ( θ ( N ) ) } \{f(\theta^{(1)}),\ldots,f(\theta^{(N)})\} is uniformly distributed over integers [ 0 , N ] [0,N] , where f : Θ → ℝ f:\Theta\to\mathbb{R} is any one-dimensional function and Θ \Theta is the sample space of θ \theta . Picking θ := v j \theta:=v_{j} and z := Y j z:=Y_{j} , where v j v_{j} and Y j Y_{j} are generated according to the dynamics–data model 2.1 , we have ( θ ~ , z ~ ) ∼ ℙ ⁡ ( v j † , Y j † ) (\tilde{\theta},\tilde{z})\sim\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j},Y^{\dagger}_{j}) . Then, we have { θ ( 1 ) , … , θ ( N ) } = { v j ( 1 ) , … , v j ( N ) } ∼ ℙ ⁡ ( v j † | Y j † ) , \{\theta^{(1)},\ldots,\theta^{(N)}\}=\{v_{j}^{(1)},\ldots,v_{j}^{(N)}\}\sim\mathbb{P}(v^{\dagger}_{j}|Y^{\dagger}_{j}), (B.71) i.e., samples drawn from the true filtering distribution. The stated result follows. ∎ Appendix C Bootstrap Particle Filter Reference and Evaluation In the low-dimensional experiments for the doubling-angle model in Subsection 4.4 and the Lorenz ’63 model in Subsection 4.5 , we use a bootstrap particle filter (BPF) with 10 6 10^{6} particles as an accurate numerical proxy for the true filtering distribution. This appendix describes how this reference filter is constructed and how its outputs are stored for later comparison. We first introduce BPF in C.1 . We then describe the recorded BPF quantities in C.2 for the comparison metric calculation. Next, we report diagnostic evidence in C.3 for the two low-dimensional models showing that the BPF is effective. Finally, we define the evaluation metric used when comparing an ensemble-based method against the BPF reference in C.4 . C.1 Bootstrap Particle Filter We consider the dynamics–observation model in ( 2.1 ). At assimilation step j j , the BPF represents the filtering distribution π j − 1 \pi_{j-1} by an equally weighted particle system of M M particles π j − 1 M = 1 M ​ ∑ m = 1 M δ v j − 1 ( m ) . \pi_{j-1}^{M}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\delta_{v_{j-1}^{(m)}}. The forecast particles are obtained by independently propagating each analysis particle through the stochastic dynamics, v ^ j ( m ) = Ψ ⁡ ( v j − 1 ( m ) ) + ξ j − 1 ( m ) , ξ j − 1 ( m ) ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) , m ∈ [ M ] . \widehat{v}_{j}^{(m)}=\Psi\bigl(v_{j-1}^{(m)}\bigr)+\xi_{j-1}^{(m)},\qquad\xi_{j-1}^{(m)}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma),\qquad m\in[M]. (C.1) The empirical predictive distribution is therefore π ^ j M = 1 M ​ ∑ m = 1 M δ v ^ j ( m ) . \widehat{\pi}_{j}^{M}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j}^{(m)}}. With the observation y j † y^{\dagger}_{j} , since the observation noise is Gaussian, the normalized BPF weights w j ( m ) w_{j}^{(m)} are w ~ j ( m ) = exp ( − 1 2 ‖ y j † − h ( v ^ j ( m ) ) ‖ Γ 2 ) , w j ( m ) = w ~ j ( m ) ∑ r = 1 M w ~ j ( r ) , m = 1 , 2 , … , M . \tilde{w}_{j}^{(m)}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left\|y_{j}^{\dagger}-h\bigl(\widehat{v}_{j}^{(m)}\bigr)\right\|_{\Gamma}^{2}\right),\quad w_{j}^{(m)}=\frac{\tilde{w}_{j}^{(m)}}{\sum_{r=1}^{M}\tilde{w}_{j}^{(r)}},\quad m=1,2,\ldots,M. (C.2) The weighted particle approximation of the filtering distribution is π ¯ j M = ∑ m = 1 M w j ( m ) ​ δ v ^ j ( m ) . \overline{\pi}_{j}^{M}=\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{j}^{(m)}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{j}^{(m)}}. To obtain an equally weighted analysis ensemble, we resample M M times from the forecast particles according to the normalized weights. Specifically, conditionally on the forecast particles and the observation history, the resampling indices satisfy ℙ ⁡ ( I j ( m ) = r ) = w j ( r ) , r ∈ [ M ] , m ∈ [ M ] . \mathbb{P}\left(I_{j}^{(m)}=r\right)=w_{j}^{(r)},\qquad r\in[M],\qquad m\in[M]. (C.3) The resampled analysis particles and the resulting empirical filtering distribution are v j ( m ) = v ^ j ( I j ( m ) ) , m ∈ [ M ] , π j M = 1 M ​ ∑ m = 1 M δ v j ( m ) . v_{j}^{(m)}=\widehat{v}_{j}^{\left(I_{j}^{(m)}\right)},\quad m\in[M],\quad\pi_{j}^{M}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\delta_{v_{j}^{(m)}}. (C.4) C.2 Recorded BPF Quantities At every assimilation step, we record two classes of quantities from the BPF. The first class consists of distributional features used to describe the BPF forecast or analysis ensemble, including means, covariances, principal-component information, and projected quantile functions. The second class consists of pre-resampling weight diagnostics used to assess whether the BPF remains reliable, including the effective sample size, and the weight abundance. For a state particle ensemble at time j j , written as { v j ( m ) } m = 1 M \{v_{j}^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M} , the empirical mean and covariance are v ¯ j M = 1 M ​ ∑ m = 1 M v j ( m ) , C j M = 1 M − 1 ​ ∑ m = 1 M ( v j ( m ) − v ¯ j M ) ⊗ ( v j ( m ) − v ¯ j M ) . \displaystyle\overline{v}_{j}^{M}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}v_{j}^{(m)},\qquad C_{j}^{M}=\frac{1}{M-1}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\bigl(v_{j}^{(m)}-\overline{v}_{j}^{M}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(v_{j}^{(m)}-\overline{v}_{j}^{M}\bigr). (C.5) For principal component analysis (PCA), we compute the eigendecomposition C j M = U j M ​ Λ j M ​ ( U j M ) ⊤ , U j M = [ u j , 1 M , … , u j , d v M ] , C_{j}^{M}=U_{j}^{M}\Lambda_{j}^{M}\left(U_{j}^{M}\right)^{\top},\qquad U_{j}^{M}=\left[u_{j,1}^{M},\ldots,u_{j,d_{v}}^{M}\right], (C.6) where the eigenvalues in Λ j M \Lambda_{j}^{M} are sorted in descending order. The centered PCA score of particle m m along the ℓ \ell -th principal direction is p j , ℓ ( m ) = ( u j , ℓ M ) ⊤ ​ ( v j ( m ) − v ¯ j M ) . p_{j,\ell}^{(m)}=\left(u_{j,\ell}^{M}\right)^{\top}\bigl(v_{j}^{(m)}-\overline{v}_{j}^{M}\bigr). (C.7) The projected quantile functions are the main objects used later for evaluation. For any one-dimensional projection direction e ∈ ℝ d v e\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} , we define the scalar projected particle by x j , e ( m ) = e ⊤ ​ v j ( m ) , m ∈ [ M ] . x_{j,e}^{(m)}=e^{\top}v_{j}^{(m)},\qquad m\in[M]. (C.8) Let i 1 , … , i M i_{1},\ldots,i_{M} be a permutation of [ M ] [M] such that the projected samples are sorted in nondecreasing order, x j , e ( i 1 ) ≤ x j , e ( i 2 ) ≤ ⋯ ≤ x j , e ( i M ) . x_{j,e}^{(i_{1})}\leq x_{j,e}^{(i_{2})}\leq\cdots\leq x_{j,e}^{(i_{M})}. (C.9) For τ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \tau\in[0,1] , the empirical projected quantile function is computed by linear interpolation along the sorted projected samples, Q j , e ​ ( τ ) = x j , e ( i a τ ) + ( r τ − a τ ) ​ ( x j , e ( i b τ ) − x j , e ( i a τ ) ) , \displaystyle Q_{j,e}(\tau)=x_{j,e}^{(i_{a_{\tau}})}+\bigl(r_{\tau}-a_{\tau}\bigr)\left(x_{j,e}^{(i_{b_{\tau}})}-x_{j,e}^{(i_{a_{\tau}})}\right), (C.10a) where r τ = 1 + ( M − 1 ) ​ τ , a τ = ⌊ r τ ⌋ , b τ = ⌈ r τ ⌉ . \displaystyle r_{\tau}=1+(M-1)\tau,\quad a_{\tau}=\lfloor r_{\tau}\rfloor,\quad b_{\tau}=\lceil r_{\tau}\rceil. (C.10b) When the time index is clear, we write Q e ​ ( τ ) Q_{e}(\tau) for Q j , e ​ ( τ ) Q_{j,e}(\tau) . We record this quantile function for every coordinate direction and every PCA direction. More precisely, with ℰ coord = { e 1 , … , e d v } , ℰ j pc = { u j , 1 M , … , u j , d v M } , ℰ j = ℰ coord ∪ ℰ j pc , \mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{coord}}=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{d_{v}}\},\quad\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{j}=\{u_{j,1}^{M},\ldots,u_{j,d_{v}}^{M}\},\quad\mathcal{E}_{j}=\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{coord}}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{pc}}_{j}, (C.11) we store Q j , e ​ ( τ k ) Q_{j,e}(\tau_{k}) for all e ∈ ℰ j e\in\mathcal{E}_{j} and all quantile levels τ k = k − 1 K − 1 \tau_{k}=\frac{k-1}{K-1} for k = 1 , … , K . k=1,\ldots,K. In our implementation, K = 257 K=257 . The PCA directions used in evaluation are always computed from the BPF reference, so all ensemble-based methods are compared against the same projected BPF targets. The weight diagnostics are computed before resampling, using the normalized weights { w j ( m ) } m = 1 M \{w_{j}^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M} . We record two complementary diagnostics: Effective sample size: ESS j \displaystyle\text{Effective sample size: }\mathrm{ESS}_{j} = ( ∑ m = 1 M ( w j ( m ) ) 2 ) − 1 , \displaystyle=\left(\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left(w_{j}^{(m)}\right)^{2}\right)^{-1}, (C.12a) Weight abundance: W A , j \displaystyle\text{Weight abundance: }W_{A,j} = exp ( − ∑ m = 1 M w j ( m ) log w j ( m ) ) . \displaystyle=\exp\left(-\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{j}^{(m)}\log w_{j}^{(m)}\right). (C.12b) The effective sample size is a standard diagnostic for weight degeneracy in sequential Monte Carlo methods ( Liu and Chen 1998 ; Del Moral et al. 2006 ) . The weight abundance is the exponential of the Shannon entropy of the normalized weights, and can be interpreted as the effective number of active particles on the entropy scale ( Jost 2006 ) . Table 6 : BPF quantities recorded at each assimilation step. The first group records distributional features of the state particles. The second group records pre-resampling diagnostics for validating the BPF reference. Category Metric Notation Definition Recorded features Mean v ¯ j M \overline{v}_{j}^{M} 1 M ​ ∑ m = 1 M v j ( m ) \frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}v_{j}^{(m)} Covariance C j M C_{j}^{M} 1 M − 1 ​ ∑ m = 1 M ( v j ( m ) − v ¯ j M ) ⊗ ( v j ( m ) − v ¯ j M ) \frac{1}{M-1}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\bigl(v_{j}^{(m)}-\overline{v}_{j}^{M}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(v_{j}^{(m)}-\overline{v}_{j}^{M}\bigr) Principal components U j M , Λ j M U_{j}^{M},\Lambda_{j}^{M} C j M = U j M ​ Λ j M ​ ( U j M ) ⊤ C_{j}^{M}=U_{j}^{M}\Lambda_{j}^{M}\left(U_{j}^{M}\right)^{\top} Projected quantile Q j , e ​ ( τ ) Q_{j,e}(\tau) Refer to ( C.10 ) BPF validity diagnostics Effective sample size ESS j \mathrm{ESS}_{j} ( ∑ m = 1 M ( w j ( m ) ) 2 ) − 1 \left(\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left(w_{j}^{(m)}\right)^{2}\right)^{-1} Weight abundance W A , j W_{A,j} exp ( − ∑ m = 1 M w j ( m ) log w j ( m ) ) \exp\left(-\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{j}^{(m)}\log w_{j}^{(m)}\right) The two diagnostics capture related but not identical aspects of the pre-resampling weight distribution. The effective sample size ESS j \mathrm{ESS}_{j} is based on the second moment of the normalized weights. It is highly sensitive to very large weights and therefore provides a conservative indicator of particle degeneracy. In the ideal case where all particles have equal weights, ESS j = M \mathrm{ESS}_{j}=M ; if one particle carries all the weight, then ESS j = 1 \mathrm{ESS}_{j}=1 . Thus, a small value of ESS j / M \mathrm{ESS}_{j}/M indicates that only a small fraction of particles effectively contributes to the likelihood-weighted approximation before resampling. The weight abundance W A , j W_{A,j} measures the effective number of active particles on the entropy scale. Compared with ESS j \mathrm{ESS}_{j} , it is less dominated by the largest few weights and instead summarizes how broadly the likelihood mass is distributed across particles. It also satisfies 1 ≤ W A , j ≤ M 1\leq W_{A,j}\leq M , with W A , j = M W_{A,j}=M for uniform weights and W A , j = 1 W_{A,j}=1 for a completely collapsed weight vector. We therefore use ESS j \mathrm{ESS}_{j} and W A , j W_{A,j} together: the former detects severe concentration of the weights, while the latter gives an entropy-based measure of the number of particles that carry non-negligible mass. In the low-dimensional experiments, consistently large values of both diagnostics provide evidence that the BPF with M = 10 6 M=10^{6} particles remains a reliable numerical proxy for the filtering distribution. C.3 BPF Validity for the Low-Dimensional Models For the two low-dimensional examples, we use the BPF reference only after checking two conditions: the pre-resampling weights should not collapse, and the resulting BPF posterior statistics should be stable with respect to the Monte Carlo seed. The first condition is assessed using the effective sample size ESS j \mathrm{ESS}_{j} (Equation ( C.12a )) and the weight abundance W A , j W_{A,j} (Equation ( C.12b )). The second condition is assessed by repeating the same BPF experiment with independent random seeds and measuring the across-seed variability of the posterior mean and covariance. For a fixed model, observation function, particle number M M , and test trajectory, let v ¯ j M , s ∈ ℝ d v \overline{v}_{j}^{M,s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}} and C j M , s ∈ ℝ d v × d v C_{j}^{M,s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}\times d_{v}} denote the BPF posterior mean and covariance at assimilation step j j from seed s = 1 , … , R s=1,\ldots,R . In our diagnostics, we use R = 12 R=12 independent seeds. For any seed-indexed scalar quantity z s z^{s} , write ⟨ z ⟩ R = R − 1 ​ ∑ s = 1 R z s \langle z\rangle_{R}=R^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{R}z^{s} . The across-seed standard error of the ℓ \ell -th component of the BPF posterior mean is se ^ v ¯ , j , ℓ M = [ 1 R ⁡ ( R − 1 ) ​ ∑ s = 1 R ( v ¯ j , ℓ M , s − ⟨ v ¯ j , ℓ M ⟩ R ) 2 ] 1 / 2 . \widehat{\mathrm{se}}_{\overline{v},j,\ell}^{M}=\left[\frac{1}{R(R-1)}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\left(\overline{v}_{j,\ell}^{M,s}-\langle\overline{v}_{j,\ell}^{M}\rangle_{R}\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}. (C.13) We define SE mean ​ ( M ) \mathrm{SE}_{\mathrm{mean}}(M) as the average of se ^ v ¯ , j , ℓ M \widehat{\mathrm{se}}_{\overline{v},j,\ell}^{M} over assimilation steps and state components. The covariance diagnostic is defined entrywise in the same way: se ^ C , j , ℓ ​ q M = [ 1 R ⁡ ( R − 1 ) ​ ∑ s = 1 R ( C j , ℓ ​ q M , s − ⟨ C j , ℓ ​ q M ⟩ R ) 2 ] 1 / 2 . \widehat{\mathrm{se}}_{C,j,\ell q}^{M}=\left[\frac{1}{R(R-1)}\sum_{s=1}^{R}\left(C_{j,\ell q}^{M,s}-\langle C_{j,\ell q}^{M}\rangle_{R}\right)^{2}\right]^{1/2}. (C.14) We define SE cov ​ ( M ) \mathrm{SE}_{\mathrm{cov}}(M) as the average of se ^ C , j , ℓ ​ q M \widehat{\mathrm{se}}_{C,j,\ell q}^{M} over assimilation steps and covariance entries. In the experiments, all reported averages are also taken over B = 64 B=64 independent test trajectories; the trajectory index is omitted from the notation above for readability. We use J = 200 J=200 assimilation steps for the doubling-angle model and J = 500 J=500 assimilation steps for the Lorenz ’63 model. We perform this diagnostic for four BPF reference settings: the doubling-angle model with cosine observation in Subsection 4.4 , and the Lorenz ’63 model in Subsection 4.5 with identity, square, and arctan observations. For each setting, we repeat the analysis for a range of particle numbers up to M = 10 6 M=10^{6} . Figure 11 summarizes the results. The first row reports the time- and trajectory-averaged ESS j \mathrm{ESS}_{j} and W A , j W_{A,j} as functions of M M . In all four settings, both diagnostics scale approximately linearly with M M , indicating that the likelihood weights retain a large active particle population before resampling. This provides evidence against severe weight collapse in the BPF reference runs. The second row reports SE mean ​ ( M ) \mathrm{SE}_{\mathrm{mean}}(M) and SE cov ​ ( M ) \mathrm{SE}_{\mathrm{cov}}(M) . The mean standard error decreases as M M increases. The covariance standard error is not strictly monotone over the tested particle numbers, but its magnitude remains small in all four settings. Together, the weight diagnostics and seed-stability diagnostics indicate that the BPF reference is numerically stable for these low-dimensional models. We therefore use the M = 10 6 M=10^{6} BPF output as the numerical reference for evaluating ensemble-based methods in the main experiments. Figure 11 : BPF validity diagnostics for the low-dimensional reference experiments. Columns correspond to the doubling-angle model with cosine observation and the Lorenz ’63 model with identity, square, and arctan observations. The first row shows the averaged pre-resampling effective sample size and weight abundance as functions of the particle number M M . The second row shows the seed standard errors of the BPF posterior mean and covariance, averaged over assimilation steps, test trajectories, and state or covariance entries. C.4 Sliced Energy-Distance Evaluation Metric We now define the metric used to compare an ensemble-based method with the BPF reference. Rather than storing the full BPF particle system for evaluation, we use the projected BPF quantile functions Q j , e Q_{j,e} . Thus, every method is compared with the same one-dimensional BPF targets along the coordinate and BPF-PCA directions e ∈ ℰ j e\in\mathcal{E}_{j} . For an ensemble-based method with analysis ensemble { v j ( n ) } n = 1 N \{v_{j}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N} , define the projected ensemble samples by x j , e ( n ) = e ⊤ ​ v j ( n ) x_{j,e}^{(n)}=e^{\top}v_{j}^{(n)} . The corresponding BPF target quantiles are q j , e , k = Q j , e ​ ( τ k ) q_{j,e,k}=Q_{j,e}(\tau_{k}) , where τ k = ( k − 1 ) / ( K − 1 ) \tau_{k}=(k-1)/(K-1) for k = 1 , … , K k=1,\ldots,K . To approximate integration over the quantile level, we use the trapezoidal weights α 1 \displaystyle\alpha_{1} = 1 2 ​ ( τ 2 − τ 1 ) , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}), α K \displaystyle\alpha_{K} = 1 2 ​ ( τ K − τ K − 1 ) , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{K}-\tau_{K-1}), (C.15) α k \displaystyle\alpha_{k} = 1 2 ​ ( τ k + 1 − τ k − 1 ) , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k-1}), α ¯ k \displaystyle\overline{\alpha}_{k} = α k ∑ ℓ = 1 K α ℓ , \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{k}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\alpha_{\ell}}, where the definition of α k \alpha_{k} in the second line applies for 2 ≤ k ≤ K − 1 2\leq k\leq K-1 . The projected ensemble law is F j , e N = N − 1 ​ ∑ n = 1 N δ x j , e ( n ) F_{j,e}^{N}=N^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{j,e}^{(n)}} , and the quantile-based BPF approximation is G j , e Q = ∑ k = 1 K α ¯ k ​ δ q j , e , k G_{j,e}^{Q}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\overline{\alpha}_{k}\delta_{q_{j,e,k}} . For this projection, the empirical one-dimensional energy distance between the projected ensemble and the projected quantile-based BPF approximation, called the sliced energy distance (SED), is SED j , e = 1 N ​ ∑ n = 1 N ∑ k = 1 K α ¯ k ​ | x j , e ( n ) − q j , e , k | − 1 2 ​ N 2 ​ ∑ n = 1 N ∑ n ′ = 1 N | x j , e ( n ) − x j , e ( n ′ ) | − 1 2 ∑ k = 1 K ∑ ℓ = 1 K α ¯ k α ¯ ℓ | q j , e , k − q j , e , ℓ | . \operatorname{SED}_{j,e}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\overline{\alpha}_{k}\left|x_{j,e}^{(n)}-q_{j,e,k}\right|-\frac{1}{2N^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{n^{\prime}=1}^{N}\left|x_{j,e}^{(n)}-x_{j,e}^{(n^{\prime})}\right|\\ -\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\overline{\alpha}_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{\ell}\left|q_{j,e,k}-q_{j,e,\ell}\right|. (C.16) The three terms are respectively the cross-distance between the method and the BPF target, the self-distance of the method, and the self-distance of the BPF target. The time-averaged SED is then SED = 1 J ​ ∑ j = 1 J 1 | ℰ j | ​ ∑ e ∈ ℰ j SED j , e . \operatorname{SED}=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}_{j}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{j}}\operatorname{SED}_{j,e}. (C.17) A smaller SED \operatorname{SED} indicates closer agreement with the BPF reference across the chosen coordinate and BPF-PCA projections. When results are reported over multiple test trajectories, the same score is computed for each trajectory and then averaged; the trajectory index is omitted above for readability. Appendix D Implementation Details This appendix provides additional implementation details for the numerical experiments in Section 4 . D.1 summarizes the neural-network architectures and training hyperparameters used by the learning-based filters. D.2 describes the grid-search protocol used to tune the classical filtering baselines. D.3 explains the observation-noise calibration procedure used to compare different observation maps under matched signal-to-noise ratios. Further implementation details, experiment scripts, and code are available in the project repository 2 2 2 https://github.com/wispcarey/Proper-Scoring-Ensemble-Filter . D.1 Neural Network Architecture and Training Hyperparameters This subsection summarizes the neural-network configurations and training hyperparameters used for the learning-based filters in the numerical experiments. The two architecture families are those introduced in Subsection 4.1 : the EnKF correction-terms architecture ( Bach et al. 2026 ) , denoted by CorrTerms, and the end-to-end filtering architecture, denoted by EtE. For each model, the same neural architecture is used for the ES-trained and NL2-trained variants; only the training objective is changed. In the linear–Gaussian diagnostic experiment in Subsection 4.3 , the reported objective comparison uses the EtE architecture with the ES, L2, and NL2 losses. Table 7 shows the neural network architecture details. The latent dimension controls the internal set representation, and the output feature dimension gives the size of the set-transformer feature representation passed to the subsequent filtering map. The seed column gives the number of learned seed vectors used by the set transformer. The attention-block column reports the total number of attention blocks, consisting of the self-attention blocks, and one bottleneck cross-attention block. Table 7 : Neural-network architecture settings for the learning-based filters. Model Architecture Total params Set-transformer params Latent dim Output feature dim Seed vectors Attention blocks Attention heads Linear–Gaussian CorrTerms 108845 47152 16 128 16 7 8 EtE 75908 Doubling-angle CorrTerms 172260 117856 32 128 16 7 8 EtE 143009 Lorenz ’63 CorrTerms 389001 334016 64 128 16 7 8 EtE 359427 Lorenz ’96 CorrTerms 404463 336960 64 128 16 7 8 EtE 368296 The training and evaluation datasets are generated independently for each model setting described in Subsections 4.3 – 4.6 . Table 8 reports the number of simulated trajectories, trajectory lengths, learning rates, batch sizes, training clamp values, and weight-decay values. All configurations use zero weight decay except the CorrTerms architecture for the Lorenz ’96 experiment, where the weight decay is set to 0.01 0.01 . Table 8 : Training and evaluation hyperparameters for the learning-based filters. Model Architecture Training trajectories Training length Test trajectories Test length Learning rate Batch size Training clamp Weight decay Linear–Gaussian CorrTerms 8192 60 64 100 1 × 10 − 3 1\times 10^{-3} 1024 – 0 EtE Doubling-angle CorrTerms 8192 60 64 200 3 × 10 − 4 3\times 10^{-4} 1024 2 0 EtE Lorenz ’63 CorrTerms 8192 60 64 500 1 × 10 − 3 1\times 10^{-3} 512 60 0 EtE Lorenz ’96 CorrTerms 8192 60 64 1500 1 × 10 − 3 1\times 10^{-3} 512 20 0.01 0.01 EtE 0 The different test lengths are chosen according to model complexity and reference-filter cost. For the linear–Gaussian model, the dynamics and filtering distribution are relatively simple, and the exact Kalman filter is available as a reference; using a much longer test trajectory provides limited additional information, so we use test length 100 100 . For the doubling-angle and Lorenz ’63 models, the reference filtering distribution is approximated by a particle filter with 10 6 10^{6} particles. Since this reference computation becomes expensive for long trajectories, we use test lengths 200 200 and 500 500 , respectively. For the Lorenz ’96 model, the state dimension is d v = 40 d_{v}=40 , making a particle filter reference computationally infeasible. We therefore use a longer test trajectory of length 1500 1500 to obtain more stable test averages for the evaluation metrics. The training clamp is used only during training. After each analysis step, if the absolute value of a state component exceeds the listed threshold, that component is clipped to the threshold with the same sign. This clipping is introduced to improve stability during the early stage of training and is not used during testing. A dash in the training-clamp column indicates that no clamp is used. The ensemble size used during training follows the setting specified in the corresponding numerical experiment. The doubling-angle model in Subsection 4.4 is trained at ensemble size N = 30 N=30 and evaluated at other ensemble sizes without fine-tuning. The Lorenz ’63 experiments in Subsection 4.5 are trained at N = 10 N=10 and evaluated directly across the tested ensemble sizes. For the Lorenz ’96 experiment in Subsection 4.6 , the initial training is performed at ensemble size N = 10 N=10 . When evaluating at a different ensemble size N ′ ≠ N N^{\prime}\neq N , we freeze the set-transformer parameters during fine-tuning, corresponding to approximately 83.3 % 83.3\% of the CorrTerms parameters and 91.5 % 91.5\% of the EtE parameters. D.2 Grid Search for Classical Filtering Baselines This subsection describes how the hyperparameters of the classical filtering baselines are selected before producing the reported test results. All classical benchmarks are intentionally tuned by grid search on the test dataset itself. That is, for each dynamical model, observation map, ensemble size, and filtering method, the classical filter is run over the candidate hyperparameter grid on the same test trajectories used for the reported metrics, and the best grid point is selected according to the corresponding test-set objective. This test-set grid search gives the classical benchmarks an additional advantage, since their inflation and localization parameters are optimized directly for the evaluation data. Nevertheless, as shown in the main numerical results, the learning-based methods, especially EtE+ES, still outperform these classical benchmarks. Figure 12 : Representative Lorenz ’63 grid search for the EnKF with ensemble size N = 10 N=10 . The three panels correspond to the partial identity, partial square, and partial arctan observation settings. Since Lorenz ’63 is low-dimensional, no localization is used, and the search is performed over the inflation factor only. The objective is SED, with smaller values indicating better agreement with the BPF reference filtering distribution. The red star marks the selected inflation factor for each observation setting. Figure 13 : Representative Lorenz ’96 grid search for localized EnKF and LETKF with ensemble size N = 10 N=10 . The columns correspond to the partial identity, partial square, and partial arctan observation settings, while the rows correspond to EnKF and LETKF. For each method and observation setting, the grid search is performed jointly over the inflation factor and the Gaspari–Cohn localization radius. The objective is RES, the relative energy score, with smaller values indicating better probabilistic filtering performance. The red star marks the selected pair of inflation factor and localization radius. All classical filters use post-analysis multiplicative inflation. We denote the inflation factor by α ≥ 1 \alpha\geq 1 . When spatial localization is used, we denote the localization radius by r r . For each ensemble size N N , we use a finite inflation grid 𝒜 N = { 1 , 1 + Δ N α , 1 + 2 ​ Δ N α , … , 1 + ( K α − 1 ) ​ Δ N α } . \mathcal{A}_{N}=\{1,1+\Delta^{\alpha}_{N},1+2\Delta^{\alpha}_{N},\ldots,1+(K_{\alpha}-1)\Delta^{\alpha}_{N}\}. (D.1) The step size Δ N α \Delta^{\alpha}_{N} and the number of candidates K α K_{\alpha} are chosen before running the grid search. Smaller ensembles are searched over a wider inflation range, while larger ensembles use milder inflation grids. For localized experiments, we also use a finite localization grid ℛ N = { ε , Δ N r , 2 ​ Δ N r , … , ( K r − 1 ) ​ Δ N r } , \mathcal{R}_{N}=\{\varepsilon,\Delta^{r}_{N},2\Delta^{r}_{N},\ldots,(K_{r}-1)\Delta^{r}_{N}\}, (D.2) where ε \varepsilon is a near-zero localization radius. Smaller ensembles use stronger localization, corresponding to smaller localization radii, while larger ensembles are allowed to use larger localization radii. For the representative N = 10 N=10 searches shown in Figures 13 and 13 , the grids are 𝒜 10 \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{10} = { 1 , 1.04 , 1.08 , 1.12 , 1.16 , 1.20 , 1.24 , 1.28 } , \displaystyle=\{1,1.04,1.08,1.12,1.16,1.20,1.24,1.28\}, ℛ 10 \displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{10} = { 0.001 , 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 } . \displaystyle=\{0.001,1,2,3,4,5\}. (D.3) For the linear–Gaussian diagnostic experiment, where the Kalman filter gives an exact reference filtering distribution, localized ensemble baselines are tuned by minimizing the Wasserstein error: ( α ⋆ , r ⋆ ) = arg ​ min α ∈ 𝒜 N , r ∈ ℛ N ⁡ W 2 ​ ( α , r ) . (\alpha^{\star},r^{\star})=\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{N},r\in\mathcal{R}_{N}}\operatorname{W}_{2}(\alpha,r). (D.4) Here W 2 ⁡ ( α , r ) \operatorname{W}_{2}(\alpha,r) denotes the Wasserstein error obtained by running the corresponding classical filter with inflation factor α \alpha and localization radius r r . For the low-dimensional nonlinear systems, namely the doubling-angle and Lorenz ’63, no localization is used. Therefore, the grid search reduces to a one-dimensional search over the inflation factor: α ⋆ = arg ​ min α ∈ 𝒜 N ​ SED ​ ( α ) . \alpha^{\star}=\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{N}}\operatorname{SED}(\alpha). (D.5) The same procedure is applied separately for each observation map, ensemble size, and classical filtering method. In these experiments, SED is computed against the BPF reference filtering distribution. Thus, the selected inflation factor is the one whose filtering ensemble is closest to the BPF reference in SED. For Lorenz ’96, localization is important for stable high-dimensional filtering. For localized baselines, we therefore perform a two-dimensional search over inflation and localization radius: ( α ⋆ , r ⋆ ) = arg ​ min α ∈ 𝒜 N , r ∈ ℛ N ​ RES ​ ( α , r ) . (\alpha^{\star},r^{\star})=\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{N},r\in\mathcal{R}_{N}}\operatorname{RES}(\alpha,r). (D.6) Here RES \operatorname{RES} denotes the relative energy score. The localized EnKF, LETKF, and IEnKF use the Gaspari–Cohn localization function ( Gaspari and Cohn 1999 ) and are tuned by the joint search in ( D.6 ). If a grid-search run diverges or produces numerically invalid filtering outputs, it is excluded from selection by assigning it an infinite objective value. Figures 13 and 13 show representative grid searches for Lorenz ’63 and Lorenz ’96. The red stars mark the selected test-set optima. These examples illustrate the tuning procedure used for the classical benchmarks; the complete grid-search configurations, including all ensemble sizes and observation settings, are provided in the project repository linked at the beginning of this appendix. The grid search is carried out independently for each classical filtering method rather than sharing hyperparameters across methods. This is important because stochastic, deterministic, localized, and iterative ensemble filters can have substantially different stability and calibration behavior under the same inflation factor. Since the grid search is performed on the test dataset, the reported classical benchmark results should be interpreted as favorably tuned test-set benchmark results rather than validation-selected results. This convention gives the classical methods a stronger comparison point, but it does not change the main conclusion: the proposed learning-based proper-scoring filter achieves better performance than the classical benchmarks even under this favorable tuning protocol. D.3 Observation-Noise Calibration by Signal-to-Noise Ratio This subsection describes how we calibrate the observation-noise standard deviation σ y \sigma_{y} when comparing different observation maps. The signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) defined here is used only to set the observation-noise level in the experiments. It is not used as an evaluation metric. The observation maps h ⁡ ( ⋅ ) h(\cdot) are constructed in two steps. We first choose the observed state dimensions, and then apply a scalar observation operation componentwise on the selected state values. For a scalar observed state value v o v^{o} , we have the default observation maps Default observation of doubling-angle: h ⁡ ( v o ) = cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ v o ) . \displaystyle h(v^{o})=\cos(2\pi v^{o}). (D.7a) Default observation of Lorenz ’63 &amp; ’96: h ⁡ ( v o ) = v o . \displaystyle h(v^{o})=v^{o}. (D.7b) Since the doubling-angle model is one-dimensional, this is a full observation. For Lorenz ’63 and Lorenz ’96, the default observation is the identity operation on each observed dimension. The nonlinear observation operations considered for Lorenz ’63 and Lorenz ’96 are Square Observation: h ⁡ ( v o ) = ( v o ) 2 , \displaystyle h(v^{o})=(v^{o})^{2}, (D.8a) Arctan Observation: h ⁡ ( v o ) = arctan ⁡ ( v o ) . \displaystyle h(v^{o})=\arctan(v^{o}). (D.8b) For vector-valued observations, ( D.7b )–( D.8b ) are applied componentwise to the observed dimension. For a fixed observation map h h , consider the observation model y j + 1 † = h ⁡ ( v j + 1 † ) + η j + 1 † , η j + 1 † ∼ 𝖭 ⁡ ( 0 , σ y 2 ​ I d y ) , j ∈ ℤ + , y_{j+1}^{\dagger}=h(v_{j+1}^{\dagger})+\eta_{j+1}^{\dagger},\quad\eta_{j+1}^{\dagger}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\sigma_{y}^{2}I_{d_{y}}),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}, (D.9) where v j † v_{j}^{\dagger} is the truth state and d y d_{y} is the observation dimension. Since different observation maps can substantially change the empirical scale of the clean observation h ⁡ ( v j † ) h(v_{j}^{\dagger}) , the same nominal value of σ y \sigma_{y} may correspond to very different effective observation-noise levels. This is particularly important when comparing identity, square, and arctan observations. Given a representative truth trajectory of length J J , we compute the time average of the clean observations and the empirical signal strength as S h = 1 J ​ ∑ j = 1 J ‖ h ⁡ ( v j † ) − h ¯ ‖ 2 2 , h ¯ = 1 J ​ ∑ j = 1 J h ⁡ ( v j † ) \displaystyle S_{h}=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left\|h(v_{j}^{\dagger})-\overline{h}\right\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\overline{h}=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}h(v_{j}^{\dagger}) (D.10) Because the observation noise covariance is σ y 2 ​ I d y \sigma_{y}^{2}I_{d_{y}} , the total observation-noise variance is S η = d y ​ σ y 2 . S_{\eta}=d_{y}\sigma_{y}^{2}. We define the empirical variance-ratio SNR by SNR ⁡ ( σ y ) = S h S η = 1 d y ​ σ y 2 ​ 1 J ​ ∑ j = 1 J ‖ h ⁡ ( v j † ) − h ¯ ‖ 2 2 . \operatorname{SNR}(\sigma_{y})=\frac{S_{h}}{S_{\eta}}=\frac{1}{d_{y}\sigma_{y}^{2}}\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left\|h(v_{j}^{\dagger})-\overline{h}\right\|_{2}^{2}. (D.11) This is a variance ratio rather than a standard-deviation ratio. Therefore, the SNR is inversely proportional to σ y 2 \sigma_{y}^{2} . In practice, we start from a default noise level σ ^ y \widehat{\sigma}_{y} and measure the corresponding default SNR for each observation map R ^ = SNR ⁡ ( σ ^ y ) = S h d y ​ σ ^ y 2 . \widehat{R}=\operatorname{SNR}(\widehat{\sigma}_{y})=\frac{S_{h}}{d_{y}\widehat{\sigma}_{y}^{2}}. If the target SNR is R R , then the noise level that achieves this target is σ y , target = σ ^ y ​ ( R ^ / R ) 1 / 2 . \sigma_{y,\mathrm{target}}=\widehat{\sigma}_{y}\left(\widehat{R}/R\right)^{1/2}. (D.12) For Lorenz ’63 and Lorenz ’96, we use the default-observation SNR as the target SNR for the square and arctan observation experiments. The calibrated value σ y ​ ( h ) \sigma_{y}(h) is therefore chosen separately for each dataset and observation map. Table 9 reports the SNR calibration settings. The left table gives the empirical variance-ratio SNR values at the baseline observation-noise level σ y \sigma_{y} . The right table gives the calibrated values of σ y ​ ( h ) \sigma_{y}(h) used for the square and arctan observation experiments on Lorenz ’63 and Lorenz ’96. All numerical values are rounded to two decimal places. Table 9 : SNR calibration settings. Left: empirical variance-ratio SNR values at the baseline observation-noise level σ y \sigma_{y} . Right: calibrated observation-noise standard deviations used to match the default-observation SNR. Empty entries indicate observation maps that are not used for that dataset. Baseline SNR Dataset 𝝈 𝒚 \bm{\sigma_{y}} Default Square Arctan Doubling-angle 0.20 0.20 12.43 12.43 – – Lorenz ’63 2.00 2.00 15.14 15.14 1090.63 1090.63 0.41 0.41 Lorenz ’96 1.00 1.00 13.28 13.28 594.67 594.67 1.01 1.01 Calibrated σ y ​ ( h ) \bm{\sigma_{y}}(h) Dataset Square Arctan Lorenz ’63 16.98 16.98 0.33 0.33 Lorenz ’96 6.69 6.69 0.28 0.28 For all experiments, filters compared under the same dataset and observation map use the same calibrated value of σ y ​ ( h ) \sigma_{y}(h) . Therefore, the SNR calibration controls the observation-noise setting, while filtering accuracy and ensemble spread are evaluated separately using the metrics described in Subsection 4.2 . Appendix E Additional Experimental Results This appendix presents additional experimental results that complement the numerical experiments in Section 4 . These results further illustrate the behavior of the proposed method and the benchmark filters on representative test trajectories, with particular emphasis on settings where the filtering distributions are strongly non-Gaussian or multimodal. E.1 Doubling-Angle Model We first provide additional visualizations for the doubling-angle model studied in Subsection 4.4 . Figures 14 and 15 compare the predictive and filtering densities at assimilation steps J = 150 J=150 and J = 200 J=200 , respectively. At both timesteps, the proposed EtE+ES method remains the closest to the bootstrap particle filter (BPF) reference. In particular, it captures the non-Gaussian and multimodal structure of both the predictive and filtering densities much more accurately than the competing methods. By contrast, the alternative machine learning models and the classical benchmark filters often fail to reproduce the correct modal structure or place sufficient mass in the relevant regions of the state space. E.2 Lorenz ’63 We next provide additional visualizations of the filtering distributions for the Lorenz ’63 model studied in Subsection 4.5 . The results are reported separately for the partial identity and partial arctan observation settings, which improves readability and allows each observation model to be discussed independently. Figure 16 shows the two-dimensional projected filtering ensembles at timesteps J = 200 J=200 and J = 300 J=300 under the partial identity observation setting. Under this observation model, EtE+ES provides the closest visual agreement with the BPF reference. CorrTerms+ES also captures part of the non-Gaussian structure, but its approximation is generally less accurate than that of EtE+ES. The methods trained with the normalized L 2 L^{2} loss, namely EtE+NL2 and CorrTerms+NL2, as well as the classical benchmark filters, show more substantial deviations from the target filtering distributions. Truth EtE CorrTerms EtE CorrTerms ESRF EnKF IEnKF BPF + ES + ES + NL2 + NL2 Partial identity observation Step 200 dim 1 , 2 1,2 Step 200 dim 1 , 3 1,3 Step 200 dim 2 , 3 2,3 Step 300 dim 1 , 2 1,2 Step 300 dim 1 , 3 1,3 Step 300 dim 2 , 3 2,3 Figure 16 : Visualization of filtering distributions for a test trajectory of the Lorenz ’63 problem under the partial identity observation setting. All machine learning methods and classical benchmark filters are evaluated at ensemble size N = 100 N=100 . EtE+ES provides the closest agreement with the BPF reference, while CorrTerms+ES gives the second-best approximation among the learning-based methods. Figure 17 reports the corresponding results under the partial arctan observation setting. Because this observation model introduces an additional nonlinear transformation, the resulting filtering problem is more challenging. Even in this case, EtE+ES remains the most accurate among the tested methods and reproduces the overall shape of the BPF reference more faithfully than the alternatives. CorrTerms+ES again captures some non-Gaussian structure, whereas the normalized- L 2 L^{2} variants and the classical benchmark filters exhibit more pronounced discrepancies.
